Gaussian Optimal Transport Beyond Brenier’s Theorem
Ho Yunabel=e1, mark]ho.yun@epfl.ch, Yoav Zemel

Abstract
We explore the geometry of the Bures-Wasserstein space for potentially degenerate Gaussian measures on a separable Hilbert space. In this general setting, the optimal transport map is formally the subgradient of a convex function that is infinite almost everywhere, rendering conventional duality-based variational methods ineffective. We overcome this analytical barrier by exploiting a constructive operator-theoretic approach. Our central result proves that the Kantorovich problem for any pair of Gaussian measures reduces to a Monge problem; that is, an optimal transport map exists in at least one direction between two measures. This reduction allows for a complete characterization with explicit formulas for all optimal (potentially unbounded) Monge transport map and Kantorovich couplings, as well as establishing their uniqueness. Furthermore, we provide a full description of the convex set of geodesics between degenerate measures, revealing a rich geometric structure where the classical McCann interpolants arise as the extreme points. We apply these findings to construct transport maps for Gaussian processes and introduce a novel framework for Wasserstein barycenters based on random Green’s operators.

[class=AMS]

1
Introduction

Optimal Transport (OT) has evolved into a central pillar of modern analysis, bridging probability, geometry, and partial differential equations. At the heart of this theory lies the celebrated theorem of Brenier, which asserts that for the quadratic cost, the unique optimal transport map between two probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(with the source being absolutely continuous) is the gradient of a convex function
[
51
,
50
,
4
]
. When restricted to the space of Gaussian measures, this geometric framework specializes to the Bures-Wasserstein geometry. Here, the transport problem between two centered Gaussian measures,
\mu=N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
and
\nu=N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{B})
, admits a symmetric positive-definite matrix
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{-1/2}
as the unique optimal pushforward, provided that the source covariance
\mathbf{A}
is non-degenerate
[
30
,
48
,
44
,
6
]
.
However, the classical theory encounters a fundamental barrier when the measures become degenerate or infinite-dimensional. In these regimes – common in the study of Gaussian processes and functional data analysis
[
17
,
43
,
35
,
31
]
– the standard tools of convex analysis fracture
[
47
]
:

1.

Singularity:
When the source covariance
\mathbf{A}
is singular (rank-deficient), the map
\mathbf{T}
is no longer uniquely defined by the gradient of a convex function almost everywhere. The transport plan may split mass, and the classical duality arguments become intractable.

2.

Unboundedness:
In infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, optimal maps between Gaussian measures often manifest as unbounded operators (e.g., differentiation). The analysis of a pushforward becomes delicate, as the domain of the linear map may have measure zero with respect to the source Gaussian measure.

1.1
Contribution

We establish a comprehensive framework for Gaussian Optimal Transport that investigates the geometry of the Bures-Wasserstein space
(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H}),\mathcal{W}_{2})
. This space consists of trace-class covariance operators on a separable Hilbert space equipped with the 2-Wasserstein distance
[
15
,
40
,
11
,
43
,
35
]
:

\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\tr(\mathbf{A})+\tr(\mathbf{B})-2\tr(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}.

Our work focuses on optimal transport between centered
Gaussian
measures, with particular emphasis on the challenges posed by
degeneracy
and
infinite-dimensionality
. In these settings, the transport map is formally the subgradient of a convex function that is infinite almost everywhere, rendering conventional approaches rooted in Kantorovich duality or Otto’s calculus intractable.

To circumvent the limitations of Brenier’s theorem, we adopt a constructive approach rooted in operator theory
[
10
,
22
,
45
,
46
]
and probability theory
[
26
,
7
,
8
]
. Our analysis centers on the concept of
linear reachability
. In the context of Gaussian optimal transport problem, we demonstrate that the existence of an optimal transport map is governed not by absolute continuity, rather by the dimensional inequality related to the covariance operators. By utilizing the Douglas factorization lemma and the Hilbertian Schur complement, we provide explicit solutions to the Monge and Kantorovich problems “beyond Brenier.”
The primary tools developed to achieve these results are (i) Green’s operators, (ii) the Hilbertian Schur complement, and (iii) admissible pre-pushforwards, in connection to (i) statistical shape analysis, (ii) conditional independence, and (iii) abstract Wiener spaces, respectively. Many of our results are new even in the finite-dimensional setting. Our main contributions are summarized as follows:

Reduction of Kantorovich to Monge

We prove that for any two centered Gaussian measures on a separable Hilbert space, the linear pre-pushforward map for the Monge problem exists if and only if the following dimensional inequality holds:

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}]\leq\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})].

We demonstrate that for any pair of covariances, this condition (or the same inequality with
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
swapped) is always satisfied. When
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the rank-nullity theorem simplifies the dimensional inequality into
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
. Consequently, an optimal transport map always exists in at least one direction as an extension of linear pre-pushfoward map, allowing the Kantorovich problem to be reduced to a Monge problem. This result effectively completes the transport picture for degenerate Gaussians and extends to general non-centered Gaussian measures via translation
[
41
]
.

Characterization of Transport Maps

To carefully address the subtleties of unbounded operators in the infinite-dimensional regime, we introduce the notion of optimal pre-pushforwards defined on dense subspaces – specifically, the reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})

[
13
,
42
]
). While this linear map itself may not be a valid transport map on the entire space, it can always be extended to yield an optimal transport map for the Monge problem. We derive necessary and sufficient conditions for the existence of optimal pre-pushforwards and provide explicit operator formulas for them. We also emphasize that, contrary to certain oversights in the literature, proving (essential) self-adjointness is significantly more challenging than demonstrating the symmetry of an unbounded optimal pre-pushforward, particularly in
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert spaces
[
10
,
22
,
45
]
. For unbounded operators, symmetry is a
strictly
weaker concept than self-adjointness.

Furthermore, we prove that the uniqueness of the optimal pre-pushforward is equivalent to the vanishing of the Schur complement,
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
.
Standard restrictions found in the literature
[
35
,
12
]
, such as injectivity (
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{0}
) or null-space inclusion (
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B})
) are subsumed by this general uniqueness category. Notably, this condition is equivalent to the existence of a semi-positive definite (s.p.d.) pre-pushforward. In particular, any s.p.d. pre-pushforward is guaranteed to be optimal; thus, if the Schur complement does not vanish, no s.p.d. transport map exists.

Geometry of Geodesics

As a consequence of our reduction results,
(\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H}),\mathcal{W}_{2})
is geodesically complete
[
32
]
, in the sense that there always exists a McCann interpolant induced by deterministic Monge maps
[
36
]
.
In the singular setting, however, the geodesic path between measures may not be unique. We provide a full characterization of the convex set of all constant-speed geodesics connecting two Gaussian measures. We identify a geometric structure where the classical McCann interpolants are precisely the extreme points of this convex set. Additionally, we generalize the underlying Kantorovich dual formulation
[
9
,
12
,
30
,
48
]
to arbitrary Gaussian measures, identifying dual convex functions that generate the supremum for the dual problem via the notion of
proper alignment
.

Application to Gaussian Processes

Applying our results to stochastic processes, we present a constructive method for determining optimal transport maps between Gaussian processes using integral kernels
[
26
,
13
]
. As a major corollary, we provide the exact value of the 2-Wasserstein distance between Integrated Brownian Motions. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first explicit result for the 2-Wasserstein distance between infinite-dimensional Gaussian measures associated with distinct differential operators.

Generalization to Barycenters

In the finite-dimensional case
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, We introduce a novel framework for the Wasserstein barycenter problem
[
1
,
2
,
53
]
and its multi-marginal generalizations, based on the concept of random Green’s matrices.

The paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
develops the necessary operator-theoretic preliminaries, introducing Green’s operators and the Hilbertian Schur complement.
Section

3
characterizes linear reachability in finite dimensions, establishing the algebraic conditions for the existence of symmetric transport maps.
Section

4
extends these results to the Wasserstein barycenter and multicoupling problems.
Section

5
rigorously treats the validity of unbounded pushforwards in Hilbert space. Finally,
Section

6
and
Section

7
present the general Hilbertian reachability theory and its application to Gaussian processes.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations

We denote a
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert space by
\mathcal{H}
, which is always assumed to be
separable
. Specifically, we reserve this notation for the finite-dimensional case, and explicitly denote it as
\mathbb{R}^{n}
whenever possible. For any closed subspace
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{H}
, we denote the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{V}
by
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{V}}
. The symbol
†
denotes the Moore-Penrose psuedoinverse. For an operator
\mathbf{T}
we denote its null space and its range by
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{T})
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{T})
.

The Banach space of bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted by
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, equipped with the operator norm
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}
. For any
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, it holds that
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{K}^{\dagger})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{K})^{\perp}
,
\mathbf{K}^{\dagger}\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{K})^{\perp}}
, and the closure of
\mathbf{K}\mathbf{K}^{\dagger}
is given by
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{K})}}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H})
the Banach space of compact operators. For
1\leq p<\infty
, the Banach space of the
p
-Schatten class operators on
\mathcal{H}
is defined as:

\mathcal{B}_{p}(\mathcal{H}):=\left\{\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H}):{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{K}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p}:=\left(\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\langle e_{l},(\mathbf{K}^{*}\mathbf{K})^{p/2}e_{l}\rangle\right)^{1/p}<+\infty\right\},

where
\{e_{l}\}_{l=1}^{\infty}
is any orthonormal basis (ONB) of
\mathcal{H}
. We also use
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
the Banach space of s.p.d. bounded operators, and similarly define
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
or
\mathcal{B}_{p}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
for
p\in[1,\infty)
.
For
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, its trace is defined as

\tr(\mathbf{K}):=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\langle e_{l},\mathbf{K}e_{l}\rangle,

which equals to its trace norm if and only if
\mathbf{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
using the singular value decomposition, see
Lemma

A.1
. For
p=2
, the
2
-Schatten class operators is commonly referred to as the Hilbert–Schmidt operators. Given
f,g\in\mathcal{H}
, the elementary tensor
f\otimes g:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is defined by
(f\otimes g)h:=\langle g,h\rangle f

[
24
,
42
]
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
is isometric and linearly isomorphic to the tensor product space
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, which is the completion of the vector space spanned by elementary tensors
[
25
]
.

2.2
Green’s Operator

The open mapping theorem states that the range of a compact operator on a Hilbert space cannot be closed unless it has a finite rank. Given this, comparing the ranges of two operators is a non-trivial task. The Douglas lemma
[
14
]
provides an invaluable tool for this purpose, and we rely on it extensively throughout the paper:

Lemma 2.1

(Douglas)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1})
be bounded operators. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})
.

2.

There exists some
c>0
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{*}\preceq c^{2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{*}
.

3.

There exists a bounded operator
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{2})
such that
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}
.

In particular,
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}\mathbf{A}
is a unique bounded operator satisfying
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{R}
in part 3 with
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{R}^{*})
, and it holds that
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}\mathbf{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\infty}=\inf\{c>0:\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{*}\preceq c^{2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{*}\}
in part 2.

Definition 2.1

(Partial Isometry)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. An operator
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
is called a
partial isometry
if
\|\mathbf{U}h\|=\|h\|
for any
h\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{U})^{\perp}
. The space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{U})^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{U})
are called the
initial space
and the
final space
, respectively.

The final space of a partial isometry
\mathbf{U}
is closed.
\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}
are projections onto the initial and final spaces, respectively.
Also, the adjoint operator
\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{U}^{\dagger}
is also a partial isometry with its initial and final spaces flipped
[
10
]
.

Definition 2.2

(Green’s Operator)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space and
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. An operator
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H})
satisfying
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{A}
is called a
Green’s operator
for
\mathbf{A}
, denoted by
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
. When
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we refer to
\mathbf{G}
as the
Green’s matrix
for
\mathbf{A}
.

The name “Green’s operator” is motivated by its connection to the theory of Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces (RKHSs). For a trace-class covariance operator
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, the space

\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})=(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}),{\left\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\rangle}_{\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})}),\quad{\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}f},{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g}\right\rangle}_{\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})}:={\left\langle{f},{g}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}},\quad f,g\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})^{\perp},

(1)

forms the RKHS associated with
\mathbf{A}

[
42
]
. The adjoint of any Green’s operator
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
provides a Hilbert space isomorphism between
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})^{\perp}
and this RKHS:
\|\mathbf{G}^{*}f\|_{\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})}=\|f\|_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})^{\perp}}
.
The Douglas lemma ensures that any Green’s operator
\mathbf{G}
for
\mathbf{A}
has the same range as its principal square root; that is,
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
. The lemma also leads to the
polar decomposition
: there exists a unique partial isometry
\mathbf{U}
with initial space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}^{*})}
(which is equivalent to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{U})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})
) and final space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G})}
such that
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
.

Example 2.1
.

When
\dim\mathcal{H}=\infty
, the partial isometry is the correct characterization for polar decomposition, as
\dim(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}))=\dim(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}))
is not necessarily true.
To illustrate this, let
\{e_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be an ONB for
\mathcal{H}
and
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\cdots>0
be a non-increasing sequence of non-zero singular values. Consider the operators:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k}(e_{k}\otimes e_{k})\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H}),\quad\mathbf{G}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k}(e_{k}\otimes e_{2k})\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}).

In this case, the polar decomposition
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
implies that
\mathbf{U}
is not injective. Hence,
\mathbf{U}
cannot be unitary or even an isometry.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

1.

Given
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
,
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
is a unique partial isometry with initial space
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})^{\perp}
, and final space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}\mathbf{U}
.

2.

If
\mathbf{U}
is a partial isometry whose final space contains
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
, then
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

Consequently, the space of Green’s operators of
\mathbf{A}
can be characterized as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{U}:\mathbf{U}\text{ is a partial isometry with final space including }\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}\}

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{U}:\mathbf{U}\text{ is a partial isometry with final space }\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}\}.

Proof of
Lemma

2.2
.

1.

Note that
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}=\tilde{\mathbf{G}}
, since
\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G})
by the Douglas lemma. Also, for any
h=\mathbf{G}^{*}f\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}^{*})
, we have

\|\mathbf{U}^{*}h\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{G}^{*}f\|^{2}=\|\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{*}f\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{G}^{*}f\|^{2}=\|h\|^{2},

hence by continuity,
\mathbf{U}^{*}
is a partial isometry with initial space including
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}^{*})}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
. On the other hand, its initial space satisfies
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{U}^{*})^{\perp}=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{U})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}^{\dagger})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
, i.e.
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
is the initial space of
\mathbf{U}^{*}
.

Now, consider
\mathbf{V}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}^{*})\to\mathcal{R}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{*}),\mathbf{G}^{*}f\mapsto\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{*}f
, which is a well-defined isometry. Then, we can extend
\mathbf{V}
to be a partial isometry with initial space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
and final space
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})^{\perp}
. However, this yields that

\mathbf{V}\mathbf{G}^{*}f=\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{*}f=\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{G}^{*}f,

thus
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{U}^{*}
agrees on a dense subset of their initial spaces, i.e.
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{U}^{*}
. This shows the uniqueness of
\mathbf{U}
as a partial isometry with initial space
\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\mathbf{G}})^{\perp}
, and final space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})^{\perp}
.

2.

Since
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}
is the projection onto the final space, we have
\tilde{\mathbf{G}}\tilde{\mathbf{G}}^{*}=\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{G}^{*})=\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{A}
.

∎

Indeed, when
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
could be chosen as an orthogonal matrix. The flexibility in choosing a Green’s operator offers significant practical advantages. From a numerical perspective, finding the principal square root
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
or its inverse often requires methods like eigenvalue decomposition or Newton-Schulz iteration, which can be computationally intensive and numerically sensitive to small spectral gaps
[
38
,
19
]
. In contrast, finding a Green’s operator can be far more efficient. In the finite-dimensional setting, a prime example of a Green’s matrix is the Cholesky factor
\mathbf{L}
from the decomposition
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\top}
, which is known to be faster and more stable to compute. However, this flexibility is also valuable for theoretical developments, particularly in stochastic processes or finding the Wasserstein barycenter. A specific choice of a Green’s operator may be more physically meaningful than the abstract square root (see examples in
Sections

7
and
4
). Thus, the ability to choose a convenient
\mathbf{G}
preserves theoretical consistency while enabling simpler proofs and more efficient, stable computations.

2.3
Hilbertian Schur Complement

The Schur complement is a fundamental concept in the study of conditional covariance, Gaussian graphical models, and residual regression, as it isolates the Gaussian structure that remains after conditioning. A main goal of this paper is to demonstrate that the Schur complement is a key component for understanding the Bures-Wasserstein geometry between two covariance operators
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
.
This subsection generalizes the Schur complement to an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, namely
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
. Crucially, we reveal that
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
depends only on the null space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})
, rather than on the specific operator
\mathbf{A}
itself.

We begin by recalling the Schur complement for a finite-dimensional square matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. Decomposing the space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n-n_{1}}
, we can write
\mathbf{B}
as a block matrix:

\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\\
\oplus\\
\mathbb{R}^{n-n_{1}}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\\
\oplus\\
\mathbb{R}^{n-n_{1}}\end{array}.

When the block
\mathbf{B}_{11}
is invertible, the Schur complement of
\mathbf{B}_{22}
with respect to
\mathbf{B}_{11}
is defined as
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{B}_{11}:=\mathbf{B}_{22}-\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{12}
.
For a non-invertible
\mathbf{B}_{11}
, we replace its inverse with
\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}
, which leads to the following block LDU decompositions of
\mathbf{B}
:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{B}_{22}-\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{12}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
-\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{11}&-\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix},

(2)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{B}_{22}-\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{12}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix},

(3)

These decompositions hold provided the range conditions
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{12})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11})
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{21}^{\top})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11})
are met, which ensures
\mathbf{B}_{12}=\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{12}
and
\mathbf{B}_{21}=\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{11}
. Notably, if
\mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}
, these range conditions are always satisfied due to Douglas’s lemma, and the rank of the Schur complement is given by

\rk(\mathbf{B}_{22}-\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}_{12})=\rk(\mathbf{B})-\rk(\mathbf{B}_{11}).

We now extend this framework to a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H})
be symmetric. To define the Schur complement of
\mathbf{B}
with respect to
\mathbf{A}
, we use the orthogonal decomposition of
\mathcal{H}
induced by
\mathbf{A}
:

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2},\quad\mathcal{H}_{1}:=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})},\quad\mathcal{H}_{2}:=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}).

Let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}
and
\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}
be the orthogonal projections onto
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, respectively.
Relative to this decomposition,
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
can be expressed as block operators:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},

where
\mathbf{A}_{11}:=\mathbf{A}\mid_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
is an injective operator, and the block components of
\mathbf{B}
are given by
\mathbf{B}_{ij}:=\mathbf{\Pi}_{i}\mathbf{B}\mid_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}:\mathcal{H}_{j}\to\mathcal{H}_{i}
for
i,j=1,2
.
Provided that the range condition
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{12})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2})
is satisfied, we define the
\mathbf{A}
-
Schur complement
of
\mathbf{B}
as the operator on
\mathcal{H}_{2}
as follows:

\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})^{*}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}):\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{2},

(4)

With this definition, we obtain the
\mathbf{A}
-block LDU decomposition for
\mathbf{B}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})^{*}&\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}&\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(5)

In standard notation, one would write
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{B}_{11}
, but we will shortly show that this is equivalent to
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{I}_{11}
as long as
\mathbf{B}_{11}
is invertible.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be a compact s.p.d. operator and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H})
a compact symmetric operator. Then,
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
if and only if

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{12})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}),\quad\mathbf{B}_{11}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\quad\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H}_{2}).

Proof of
Lemma

2.3
.

(
\Rightarrow
)

Immediate from the Douglas lemma
[
14
]
and the Baker theorem
[
5
,
24
]
.

(
\Leftarrow
)

Immediate from the
\mathbf{A}
-block LDU decomposition in (
5
).

∎

The following lemma formalizes the utility of the Green’s operator by providing an alternative characterization of the
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. Let
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{11})
be a Green’s operator, and denote its trivial extension to
\mathcal{H}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}.

Then the following statements hold:

1.

\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
is injective, and its extension
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H})
is a Green’s operator for
\mathbf{A}
.

2.

On
\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}=\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G}
, hence
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{G})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

3.

The Schur complement
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
can also be expressed as:

\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}_{22}-[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})]^{*}[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})],

for any
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{11})
.

4.

The operator
\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}
is block-diagonal, where its restriction to
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is the
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement:

\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{0},&\quad\text{on }\mathcal{H}_{1},\\
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A},&\quad\text{on }\mathcal{H}_{2},\end{cases}

hence
\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.4
.

1.

Since
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{11})=\{0\}
, thus
\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}
is injective. Then,
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{A}
is trivial by direct calculation.

2.

Note that
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})
. On
\mathcal{H}_{1}
,

\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}=(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})=\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G},

hence
h\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}
if and only if
h\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{G})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

3.

Let
\mathbf{Q}_{11}:=\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
. Also, since
\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, there exists some
\mathbf{R}_{12}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1})
with
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{R}_{12}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\infty}\leq 1
such that
\mathbf{B}_{12}=\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{22}^{1/2}
and
\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{22}^{1/2}=\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}

[
5
]
. Because

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{Q}_{11}^{*})^{\perp}=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{Q}_{11})},

we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})+(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{21})^{*}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{Q}_{11})}})\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{21})^{*}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{Q}_{11})}}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12},

which is a trivial extension of

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{22}-\mathbf{B}_{22}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{11}(\mathbf{Q}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{11})^{\dagger}\mathbf{Q}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{22}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{22}^{1/2})^{*}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{Q}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{11})^{\dagger}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{B}_{22}^{1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}).

From the last line, it becomes clear that its trivial extension
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
to the entire space
\mathcal{H}
can also be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}_{22}-[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})]^{*}[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})].

4.

Using
\mathbf{B}_{ij}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{i}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{j}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}=(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})=[(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{*}]](\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})^{*}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})^{*}[\mathbf{I}-[(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{*}]^{*}[(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})^{*}]](\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})^{*}[\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})}}](\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}+\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})^{*}[\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})}}](\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}+\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}[\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})}}]\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}\mathbf{B}^{1/2},

where we used
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\Pi}_{1})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*})
in the last equality. On
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}_{2}^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\perp}
, one can easily show that
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=\mathbf{0}
, hence
\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=\mathbf{0}
.
Applying the Douglas lemma, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})})=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})].

Note that
h\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]
if and only if there exists some
f\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})
such that
h=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}f
, which is equivalent to
h\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

∎

The
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement is a fundamentally algebraic construct, as it depends solely on the null space of
\mathbf{A}
rather than the geometry induced by its range. Consequently, if two operators
\mathbf{A},\tilde{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
share the same null space, their corresponding Schur complements are identical, regardless of any other differences between them. The equivalent characterizations provided in
Lemma

2.4
are therefore crucial for the development of our theory.

Corollary 2.5
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a s.p.d. matrix and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
a symmetric matrix with
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{12})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11})
. Then,

\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})=\rk(\mathbf{B})-\rk(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G}),

independent of the choice of a Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof of
Corollary

2.5
.

Define the s.p.d. block matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}:

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}&(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}

where we used
\mathbf{C}/\mathbf{I}_{11}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{I}_{11}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
from part 3 in
Lemma

2.4
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{B})=\rk(\mathbf{C})

\displaystyle=\rk(\mathbf{C}_{11})+\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=\rk(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})+\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})=\rk(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})+\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}).

∎

3
Euclidean Reachability

This section defines and characterizes the concept of linear reachability between two covariance matrices in a finite-dimensional Euclidean space to address the Monge problem
[
51
,
41
,
4
]
.

Definition 3.1

(Reachability in Finite Dimension)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices. We say that
\mathbf{B}
is linearly reachable from
\mathbf{A}
, denoted
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, if there exists a matrix
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfying:

Transportability :

\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}=\mathbf{B}
.

Optimality :

\tr(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T})=\tr[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}]
for some (and thus any) Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

Such a matrix
\mathbf{T}
is called an Optimal Transport (OT) matrix.

Note that the set of all matrices of the form
\{\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G}\mid\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{A}\}
consists of unitarily equivalent matrices due to the polar decomposition, meaning the optimality condition is not affected by the choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
. Furthermore, because the transportability condition implies that
(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G}
,
the optimality condition can be simplified to require either
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}
or
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}\succeq\mathbf{0}
due to
Lemma

A.1
.
The following theorem shows that
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
if and only if
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
. The theorem also provides a complete characterization of the set of all OT matrices that map
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
in this case.

Theorem 3.1

(Reachability)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices.

1.

Invertible Case:
If
\mathbf{A}\succ\mathbf{0}
, then
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
and the OT matrix is unique:

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}:=(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}^{-1}\succeq\mathbf{0}.

(6)

While (
6
) is independent of the choice of Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
, setting
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
yields the well-known Pusz–Woronowicz formula
[
44
]
.

2.

Singular Case:
If
\mathbf{A}
is non-invertible with
\rk(\mathbf{A})<n
, we use the block decomposition based on the orthogonal subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})
:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{T}_{11}&\mathbf{T}_{12}\\
\mathbf{T}_{21}&\mathbf{T}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
if and only if
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
. In this case,
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is an OT matrix if and only if

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}&\mathbf{T}_{12}\\
[\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}+(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}]\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}&\mathbf{T}_{22}\end{pmatrix}

where:

•

\mathbf{T}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{T}_{22}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
are arbitrary submatrices.

•

\mathbf{G}_{11}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
is any Green’s submatrix for
\mathbf{A}_{11}
, hence invertible,

•

\mathbf{U}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
is a partial isometry mapping from
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})
to a subspace
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
with the same dimension, i.e.,
\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{12}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
and
\mathbf{U}_{12}\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

This construction is independent of the choice of Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{11})
. Additionally,
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
can always be made to be symmetric via
\mathbf{T}_{12}=\mathbf{T}_{21}^{*}
and
\mathbf{T}_{22}=\mathbf{T}_{22}^{*}
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

Denote
n_{1}:=\rk(\mathbf{A})\leq n
. First, it is clear that if
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
, then
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
. Conversely, assuming
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
, we prove that
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
.

1.

If
n_{1}=n
, i.e.
\mathbf{A}\succ\mathbf{0}
, note that
\rk(\mathbf{G}^{*})=\rk(\mathbf{G})=n
as
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}^{*})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})
, so
\mathbf{G}^{*}
and
\mathbf{G}
are both full-rank. It is straightforward that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}
is an OT matrix, and is independent of the choice of
\{\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\mid\mathbf{G}\mathbf{G}^{*}=\mathbf{A}\}
, as
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G}
are unitarily equivalent.

To show the uniqueness, let
\mathbf{T}
be any transport matrix from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
. Then, rewriting the transport condition gives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}=\mathbf{B}\ \Leftrightarrow\ (\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})^{*}=\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G}.

Applying
Lemma

A.1
, a version of the von-Neumann trace inequality
[
23
]
, we have

\tr(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T})=\tr(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})\leq\tr[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}]

and equality holds if and only if
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}
, i.e.
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}
as
\mathbf{G}^{*}
and
\mathbf{G}
are both full-rank. This establishes the uniqueness of the OT matrix.

2.

Let
n_{1}<n
. Note that for any unitary matrix
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, the transformation

\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}=\mathbf{B}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{U})(\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{U})(\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{T}^{*}\mathbf{U})=\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{U}

preserves the defining property for the optimality of transport matrices. In this regard, we may decompose
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{T}
, and
\mathbf{B}
in block form as:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{T}_{11}&\mathbf{T}_{12}\\
\mathbf{T}_{21}&\mathbf{T}_{22}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix},

(7)

where
\mathbf{A}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
is p.d.. Note that
\tr(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T})=\tr(\mathbf{A}_{11}\mathbf{T}_{11})
and
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}
do not depend on
\mathbf{T}_{12}
and
\mathbf{T}_{22}
. Choose a Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
for
\mathbf{A}_{11}
.
As the optimality condition only depends on
\mathbf{T}_{11}
in
\mathbf{T}
, we can fix

\mathbf{T}_{11}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{11}\to\mathbf{B}_{11}}=(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1},

which is the only choice to be optimal from part 1. Hence,
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
if and only if there exists a
\mathbf{T}_{21}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-n_{1})\times n_{1}}
satisfying the transportability condition given by the following system of block equations:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}(\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{11}\to\mathbf{B}_{11}}\mathbf{A}_{11}\mathbf{T}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{12},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})(\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}=\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}\mathbf{T}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{22}.

To simplify this system, we introduce a change of variables.
Define the matrix
\mathbf{M}_{21}:=\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-n_{1})\times n_{1}}
. The existence of
\mathbf{T}_{21}
is equivalent to the existence of
\mathbf{M}_{21}
, which must satisfy the transformed system:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{22}.

(8)

Using the Douglas lemma, observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{12})\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2})\quad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2})=\mathcal{R}((\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}),

so the first equation in (
8
) always has a solution, characterized by

\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})+\mathbf{N}_{12},\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}((\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}).

(9)

Substituting
\mathbf{N}_{12}
into the second equation of (
8
), we obtain from part 3 in
Lemma

2.4
that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\succeq\mathbf{0}.

(10)

Define the s.p.d. block matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}:

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}&(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}

where the last equality holds as in (
3
) due to the Douglas lemma and
\mathbf{C}/\mathbf{I}_{11}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{I}_{11}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
from part 3 in
Lemma

2.4
.
Since
\rk(\mathbf{C})=\rk(\mathbf{B})\leq n_{1}
, applying
Lemma

A.2
guarantees the existence of such
\mathbf{N}_{12}
, i.e.
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
. Note that
\mathbf{N}_{12}
is characterized by
\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}
,
where
\mathbf{U}_{12}
is a partial unitary matrix satisfying
\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{12}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
and
\mathbf{U}_{12}\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{V}}
for some
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{C}_{11})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
of dimension
\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})
. Plugging these results back into
\mathbf{T}_{21}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{21}

\displaystyle=[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}+\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]^{*}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}+(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}]\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}.

Finally, we can always make an OT matrix
\mathbf{T}
to be symmetric by taking
\mathbf{T}_{12}:=\mathbf{T}_{21}^{*}
and
\mathbf{T}_{22}
be any symmetric submatrix.

∎

Remark 3.1
.

Theorem

3.1
reveals that the
reachability
relation
\to
defines a
total preorder
on the set of s.p.d. matrices, determined entirely by their rank. For any s.p.d. matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
:

1.

(Reflexivity)
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{A}
, since
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{A})
is always true.

2.

(Transitivity) If
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, then
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

3.

(Strong Connectivity) For any pair
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
, either
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
or
\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{A}
.

We now examine the evolution of rank along a geodesic path that arises from an OT matrix
\mathbf{T}
from
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
. , namely the
McCann interpolant
:

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}:=[(1-t)\mathbf{I}+t\mathbf{T}]\mathbf{A}[(1-t)\mathbf{I}+t\mathbf{T}]^{*},\quad t\in[0,1].

(11)

A direct calculation show that
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
with an OT matrix
[(1-t)\mathbf{I}+t\mathbf{T}]
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=t\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}),\quad\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t},\mathbf{B})=(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}).

A direct consequence of the reachability condition is that rank must be non-increasing in
t\in[0,1]
. As we will show later, the geodesic path is generally not unique unless stringent conditions hold. While this might suggest that rank evolution could vary, the following theorem reveals a universal property: regardless of which OT matrix is chosen, the rank along its corresponding McCann interpolant remains constant, except for the endpoint
\mathbf{B}
:

Theorem 3.2

(Constant Rank along Interpolant)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices with
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
so that
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
. Consider a McCann interpolant in (
11
), where
\mathbf{T}
is any OT matrix from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
. Then for any
t\in[0,1)
, the
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement vanishes, i.e.,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
, which implies that the rank is constant for the entire open interval, i.e.,
\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=\rk(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

We may decompose
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{T}
, and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
in block form as in (
7
). Note that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}=[((1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t\mathbf{T}_{11})\mathbf{G}_{11}][((1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t\mathbf{T}_{11})\mathbf{G}_{11}]^{*},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}=t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{21}+t(1-t)\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}=t\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}((1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t\mathbf{T}_{11}),

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}=t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{22}=t^{2}\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{T}_{21}^{*},

where
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
is a Green’s submatrix for
\mathbf{A}_{11}
.
Note that if
t<1
, then
\mathbf{X}_{11}:=(1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t\mathbf{T}_{11}\succ\mathbf{0}
is invertible since
\mathbf{T}_{11}\succeq\mathbf{0}
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}-(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}^{*}

\displaystyle=t^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})\left[\mathbf{I}_{11}-\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{X}_{11}\mathbf{X}_{11}^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}\right](\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}

\displaystyle=t^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})\left[\mathbf{I}_{11}-\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}\mathbf{G}_{11}\right](\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}=t^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})\left[\mathbf{I}_{11}-\mathbf{I}_{11}\right](\mathbf{T}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}=\mathbf{0},

since
\mathbf{G}_{11}
is invertible.
Therefore, using
Corollary

2.5
, we conclude

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=\rk(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{G})

\displaystyle=\rk(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})

\displaystyle=\rk(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}((1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t\mathbf{T}_{11})\mathbf{G}_{11})=\rk(\mathbf{G}_{11})=\rk(\mathbf{A}).

∎

In the classical Monge-Kantorovich problem with a quadratic cost, if the source measure is absolutely continuous, the unique optimal transport (OT) map is the gradient of a convex function
[
9
,
12
,
48
]
. For Gaussian measures, this implies that when the source covariance matrix
\mathbf{A}
is invertible, the OT matrix is necessarily s.p.d.
[
50
,
41
]
. However,
Theorem

3.1
establishes the existence of an OT matrix even when
\mathbf{A}
is rank-deficient. This raises a crucial question for the singular case:
When does an s.p.d. OT matrix exist on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
?

The following theorem answers this question by establishing a set of equivalent conditions. These equivalences except for the first condition also hold in the infinite-dimensional setting (
Theorem

6.7
).

Theorem 3.3

(SPD Reachability)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices. Define the set of s.p.d. transport matrices as:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{+}:=\{\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\mid\mathbf{T}\succeq\mathbf{0},\,\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}=\mathbf{B}\}.

The following statements are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{+}\neq\emptyset
.

2.

The OT matrix from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
is
N(0,\mathbf{A})
-a.s. unique.

3.

\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
.

4.

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A})
.

5.

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

Moreover, when any of these conditions hold, any s.p.d. transport matrix in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{+}
is an OT matrix. A canonical choice for this matrix, which has minimal rank of
\rk(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, can be constructed as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}:=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}&(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}&\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{2\dagger}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}.

This construction is independent of the choice of Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{11})
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.3
.

First, if a transport matrix
\mathbf{T}
from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
is s.p.d., then it is optimal due to
Lemma

A.1
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
being s.p.d.. Also, it is straightforward due to the polar decomposition that
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}
in (
3.3
) does not depend on the choice of Green’s matrix
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
. We show the rest of the statements. Following the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, we consider
\mathbf{A}
in block form as:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{A}_{11}\succ\mathbf{0}
is invertible.

(
4\Leftrightarrow 5)

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A})\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B})\ \Leftrightarrow\ \mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

(
3\Leftrightarrow 5)

Since
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})
, this is a direct consequence of part 4 in
Lemma

2.4
.

(
2\Leftrightarrow 3)

Due to
Theorem

3.1
, the OT matrix
\mathbf{T}
is
N(0,\mathbf{A})
-a.s. unique if and only if
\mathbf{T}_{21}
is uniquely determined, which in turn happens if and only if
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=0
, otherwise
-\mathbf{U}_{12}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times(n-n_{1})}
is also a partial unitary submatrix satisfying
\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{U}_{12}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
and
\mathbf{U}_{12}\mathbf{U}_{12}^{*}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

(
3\Rightarrow 1)

From the proof of
Theorem

3.1
,
\mathbf{S}:=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}
in (
3.3
) is an OT matrix, hence it remains to show that this is a s.p.d. matrix. Fix
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11})
. Then,

\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}]\quad\Rightarrow\quad(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{1}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{21}).

Thus,
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{21})\supset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11})
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{S}_{12})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{S}_{11}^{1/2})
, and thus,
\mathbf{S}/\mathbf{A}
is well-defined. Now, applying part 3 in
Lemma

2.4
yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}/\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}_{22}-(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{S}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{S}_{12})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{2\dagger}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})=\mathbf{0}.

Hence,
\mathbf{S}\succeq\mathbf{0}
from
Lemma

2.3
.

(
1\Rightarrow 3)

We may assume that
\mathbf{A}
is not injective, otherwise there is nothing to prove. Let
\mathbf{S}
be a s.p.d. OT matrix. From the proof of
Theorem

3.1
,
\mathbf{S}_{11}
must take the form

\mathbf{S}_{11}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{11}\to\mathbf{B}_{11}}=(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}.

Also, since
\mathbf{S}\succeq\mathbf{0}
, we get from
Lemma

2.3
that

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11}^{1/2})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{S}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{S}_{12})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{21}),

which leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{S}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}),

or equivalently,
\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}]\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\perp}
. Due to (
9
), there exists a partial unitary matrix
\mathbf{U}_{12}
from
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})
to
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
such that

(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{*}-(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})=\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}.

However, the range of the left hand side is included in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\perp}
, whereas the range of the left hand side belongs to
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
. Hence, we have

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{U}_{12})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2})=\{0\},

i.e.
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=0
.

Finally, the canonical choice of minimal rank given in (
3.3
) follows from the part (
3\Rightarrow 1)
.
∎

The preceding theorems provide a complete picture of reachability in this setting.
Theorem

3.1
shows that an OT matrix from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
exists if and only if
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
.
Theorem

3.3
further reveals that if any s.p.d. matrix
\mathbf{T}
satisfies the transport equation
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}=\mathbf{B}
, then it is automatically an optimal solution. Even more, it guarantees that these solutions are unique in an almost-sure sense, meaning that they are all equivalent on the support
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})
of the source measure
N(0,\mathbf{A})
. The only requirement to verify this uniqueness is the condition
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
, which entails
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
. When this condition holds for a singular
\mathbf{A}
, the proof of the part (
3\Rightarrow 1)
in
Theorem

3.3
fully characterizes the set of all s.p.d. transport matrices:

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{+}=\left\{\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{S}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}|\ \mathbf{S}_{22}\succeq\mathbf{0}\right\},

where
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}
is defined in (
3.3
). The arbitrary component
\mathbf{S}_{22}
on the null space of
\mathbf{A}
represents the degrees of freedom that do not affect optimality, which is precisely the
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement of the resulting matrix
\mathbf{S}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{S}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{S}_{22}
.

Example 3.1
.

Consider the following rank-deficient covariance matrices:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}4&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&4&2\\
0&2&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{C}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

1.

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
:
Here,
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})=\spann\{(0,2,1)^{\top}\}
intersects trivially with
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\spann\{\mathbf{e}_{3}\}
. According to
Theorem

3.3
, this guarantees the existence of an s.p.d. OT matrix, which has the following form:

\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&*\\
0&2&*\\
0&1&*\end{pmatrix},

making all such maps
N(0,\mathbf{A})
-a.s. equivalent. The canonical s.p.d. OT matrix of minimal rank in (
3.3
) is given by

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&2&1\\
0&1&1/2\end{pmatrix}.

2.

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}
:
In this case,
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{C})=\mathrm{span}\{\mathbf{e}_{3}\}
has a non-trivial intersection with
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\mathrm{span}\{\mathbf{e}_{3}\}
, and
Theorem

3.3
predicts that no s.p.d. OT matrix exists and the solution is not
N(0,\mathbf{A})
-a.s. unique. Indeed, the general form of an OT matrix takes the form:

\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{C}}^{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&*\\
0&0&*\\
(\cos\theta)/2&\sin\theta&*\end{pmatrix},\quad\theta\in[0,2\pi).

where the dependence on
\theta
confirms the non-uniqueness, and
Lemma

A.3
verifies that they do not admit an s.p.d. completion.
The associated McCann interpolant is given by

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}4(1-t)^{2}&0&2t(1-t)\cos\theta\\
0&(1-t)^{2}&t(1-t)\sin\theta\\
2t(1-t)\cos\theta&t(1-t)\sin\theta&t^{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad t\in[0,1].

For any
t\in[0,1)
, its range is

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\theta})=\mathrm{span}\left\{\begin{pmatrix}2(1-t)\\
0\\
t\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
(1-t)\\
t\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\theta})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
, or equivalently,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\theta}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
by
Theorem

3.3
. This confirms
Theorem

3.2
:
\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\theta})=\rk(\mathbf{A})=2
.

Example 3.2
.

Consider the following rank-deficient covariance matrices:

\mathbf{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\rk(\mathbf{A})=\rk(\mathbf{B})=1
, both
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{A}
are possible due to
Theorem

3.1
. The OT matrices takes the form:

\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}0&*\\
\pm 1&*\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{A}}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}*&\pm 1\\
*&0\end{pmatrix}.

Neither of these matrices allows for an s.p.d. completion, as predicted by
Theorem

3.3
:
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\spann\{\mathbf{e}_{2}\}
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B})=\mathrm{span}\{\mathbf{e}_{1}\}
.
There are only two possible McCann interpolants from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
(or vice versa) constructed from these OT matrices:

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}(1-t)^{2}&\pm t(1-t)\\
\pm t(1-t)&t^{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad t\in[0,1].

Consistent with
Theorem

3.2
, the rank of these matrices is constantly
1
along the geodesic. While these are the only possible Monge geodesics (McCann interpolants), other Kantorovich geodesics exist. In
Example

4.2
, we show that
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{I}/4
is a midpoint between
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
.
Because
\rk(\mathbf{C})=2>1=\rk(\mathbf{A})=\rk(\mathbf{B})
, we get
\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{B}
. Hence, we can construct a geodesic with
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}=\mathbf{A},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1/2}=\mathbf{C},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}=\mathbf{B}
, while there exists no single OT matrix
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}
or
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{A}}
that describes this geodesic.

4
Wasserstein Barycenters

Given a s.p.d. matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
, the SVD decomposition leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{G}\mathbf{U}:\mathbf{U}\in O(n)\}.

(18)

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices. Then, for any
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{1})
, there exists some
\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{2})
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\succeq\mathbf{0}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\tr(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2})=\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]=\tr[(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

Proof of
Lemma

4.1
.

Given
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{1})
, there exists a unitary matrix
\mathbf{U}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that
\mathbf{G}_{1}=\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}_{1}
. Then,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}=\mathbf{U}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}_{1}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]=\tr[(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

Considering the SVD decomposition
\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{V}^{*}
,
\mathbf{G}_{2}:=\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{V}^{*}
is a desired solution.
∎

In this section, we show that the Wasserstein barycenter of the random covariance matrix is equivalent to maximizing the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of the associated random Green’s matrix, in favor of shape analysis. Let
\mathscr{A}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random covariance matrix, i.e.,
\mathscr{A}(\omega)\succeq\mathbf{0}
for any
\omega\in\Omega
, satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}=\int_{\Omega}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}(\omega)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)<+\infty,

so that
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\mathbf{B})
is well-defined for any s.p.d. matrices
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
:
\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\mathbf{B})\leq 2\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+2{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{B}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<\infty
. In this case, its
Wasserstein barycenter
denoted by
\hat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, is an s.p.d. matrix that is a solution to the following minimization problem:

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\hat{\mathbf{A}},\mathscr{A})=\inf_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathscr{A}).

We are only formulating at the population level, but this includes the weighted empirical version if we consider
\mathscr{A}
having only finite number of values, say
\{\mathbf{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{A}_{m}\}
.

4.1
Random Green’s Matrix

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathscr{A}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random covariance matrix with
\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<+\infty
. We then say that
\mathscr{G}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a
random Green’s matrix
associated to
\mathscr{A}
if
\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathscr{G}(\omega)^{*}=\mathscr{A}(\omega)
for
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.. We abuse the notation
\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
to denote the space of random Green’s matrices with respect to
\mathscr{A}
. Additionally,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is said to be

1.

properly aligned
if
(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}\mathscr{G}(\omega)\succeq\mathbf{0}
(or equivalently,
(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}\mathscr{G}(\omega)=(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}^{*}\mathscr{A}(\omega)\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{1/2}
) holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s..

2.

optimal
if
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}=\max_{\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}
.

Note that the expectation
\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
of the random Green’s matrix is well-defined via Bochner integral with
(\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2})^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}<\infty
.
We also remark both the proper alignment and the optimality are right unitary invariant as in (
18
): for any unitary operator
\mathbf{U}\in O(n)
,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is properly aligned (resp. optimal) if and only if
\mathscr{G}\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
as well:

\displaystyle(\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathbf{U})(\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathbf{U})^{*}=\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathscr{G}(\omega)^{*},\quad\mathbb{E}(\mathscr{G}\mathbf{U})^{*}\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathbf{U}.

(19)

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\mathscr{A}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random covariance matrix with
\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<+\infty
.

1.

For any
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
\mathbb{E}[{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}]<+\infty
.

2.

If the random Green’s matrix
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is properly aligned, then
\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}
is a solution to

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}].

(20)

3.

If
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfies
\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{A}(\omega))
holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., then there exists a properly aligned
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
such that
\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}
.

Proof of
Proposition

4.2
.

1.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\mathbb{E}[{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}]=\mathbb{E}[{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}^{1/2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}]\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{M}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\mathbb{E}[{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}^{1/2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}]\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{M}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}]}<+\infty.

2.

(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}\mathscr{G}]=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}^{*}\mathscr{A}\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{1/2}]
.

3.

Define a random matrix
\mathscr{G}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by
\mathscr{G}(\omega):=(\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}(\omega)\mathbf{M}^{*})^{1/2}
. Then, whenever
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{A}(\omega))
holds, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathscr{G}(\omega)^{*}=[(\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\dagger}\mathbf{M}^{*}]\mathscr{A}(\omega)\overline{\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{\dagger}}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{M})}}\mathscr{A}(\omega)\mathbf{\Pi}_{\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{M})}}=\mathscr{A}(\omega),

hence,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
. Note that

\mathcal{R}[\mathscr{G}(\omega)]\subset\mathcal{R}((\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\dagger})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\perp}\,\Rightarrow\,\mathcal{R}[\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}]\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\perp}\,\Rightarrow\,\mathcal{R}[\mathbf{M}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}]\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\perp},

while

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]=\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{R}[\mathbf{M}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}]\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*}).

This yields that
\mathcal{R}[\mathbf{M}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}]\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M}^{*})=\{0\}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}
. Accordingly,

\displaystyle(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}\mathscr{G}(\omega)=\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{G}(\omega)=(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathscr{A}(\omega)\mathbf{M}^{*})^{1/2}\succeq\mathbf{0},\quad\mathbb{P}-\text{a.s.},

which shows that
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is properly aligned.

∎

In the following theorem, we will shortly see that any Green’s matrix
\mathbf{M}
of a Wasserstein barycenter
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
satisfies (
20
). In this regard, observe the peculiar order of the (LHS) in (
20
) while
\hat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}
. However, the proper alignment is unitarily invariant, so by taking
\mathbf{M}=\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2}
, we recover a well-known fixed-point equation, which is a necessity condition of the barycenter
[
30
,
1
,
49
,
35
,
31
,
2
]
:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2}\mathscr{A}\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

(21)

To discuss the Wasserstein barycenter problem, it is convenient to introduce the following risk: for
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
, we consider

\displaystyle L(\mathbf{M},\mathscr{G}):=\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}-\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}-2\tr(\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})+\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1},

\displaystyle L(\mathbf{M}):=\min_{\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}L(\mathbf{M},\mathscr{G})=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*},\mathscr{A}).

The minimum is achieved due to
Lemma

4.1
. The following statements are straightforward:

•

L(\mathbf{M},\mathscr{G})=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}
.

•

L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})
if and only if
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is properly aligned.

Again, observe the right unitary invariance
L(\mathbf{M})=L(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{U})
for any
\mathbf{U}\in O(n)
, thus the Wasserstein barycenter problem can be formulated as:

\hat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*},\quad\mathbf{M}\in\argmin_{\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}L(\mathbf{G}),

and the right unitary evolution of
\mathbf{M}
does not alter the resulting barycenter. Now, we fully characterize the barycenter problem:

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random covariance matrix with
\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<+\infty
.

1.

\hat{\mathbf{A}}\succeq\mathbf{0}
is a barycenter of
\mathscr{A}
if and only if
\hat{\mathbf{A}}=(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}
for some optimal random Green’s matrix
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
. Consequently, if the optimal random Green’s matrix does not exist, neither does the barycenter.

2.

Any optimal random Green’s matrix is properly aligned.

Proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

1. (
\Rightarrow
)

Let
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
be an optimal random Green’s matrix. For any
\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and the random Green’s matrix
\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
,

\displaystyle L(\mathbf{G},\mathscr{S})

\displaystyle=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S})+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\geq L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S})=-{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}

\displaystyle\geq-{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})\geq L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}),

hence
L(\mathbf{G})=\inf_{\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}L(\mathbf{G},\mathscr{S})\geq L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})
. Therefore,
\hat{\mathbf{A}}=(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}
is a barycenter.

1. (
\Leftarrow
)

Conversely, let
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
be a barycenter. Because of
Lemma

4.1
, there exists some
