\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
such that
L(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2},\mathscr{G})=L(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2})
. Then,

\displaystyle L(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2})=L(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2},\mathscr{G})

\displaystyle=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}\geq L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq L(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2})+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2}-\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},

thus
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{1/2}=\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}
and
L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})
, i.e.,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is properly aligned. Then, the optimality of
\mathscr{G}
follows from [1. (
\Rightarrow
)].

2.

From [1. (
\Rightarrow
)], we have

\displaystyle L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})=\inf_{\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{S})\geq L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})\geq L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}),

hence
L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G},\mathscr{G})=L(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})
, i.e.,
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is properly aligned.

∎

Remark 4.1
.

As an immediate consequence, we have
\hat{\mathbf{A}}=(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}\preceq\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{G}\mathscr{G}^{*}]=\mathbb{E}\mathscr{A}
due to the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
[
6
]
. Also, the Mincowski determinant inequality yields the result in
[
1
]
:

\displaystyle\det[\hat{\mathbf{A}}]^{\frac{1}{2n}}=\det[\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}]^{1/n}\geq\mathbb{E}[\det(\mathscr{G})^{1/n}]=\mathbb{E}[\det(\mathscr{A})^{\frac{1}{2n}}].

A careful inspection of the proof of
Theorem

4.3
reveals that the Fréchet variance is given by

\inf_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathscr{A})=-\sup_{\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1},

regardless of the existence of the barycenter.
Additionally, it is well-known that the set of barycenters is convex due to the weak convexity
[
41
,
3
]
:

\displaystyle(1-t)\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{0})+t\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{A},\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{1})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{A},(1-t)N(0,\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{0})+tN(0,\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}))

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathscr{A},(1-t)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{0}+t\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{1}),\quad t\in(0,1).

On the other hand, the set of optimal random Green’s matrix is never closed under the convex combination, except for the trivial case
\mathscr{G}_{0}=\mathscr{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
:

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(1-t)\mathscr{G}_{0}+t\mathscr{G}_{1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=(1-t){|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{G}_{0}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+t{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{G}_{1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}-t(1-t){|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{G}_{0}-\mathscr{G}_{1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},\quad t\in(0,1).

The existence of a weighted barycenter is guaranteed when a random covariance matrix
\mathscr{A}
only takes finite values, say
\mathbf{A}_{i}
with weights
p_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,m
. This is because the expected trace is finite,
\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\tr(\mathbf{A}_{i})<+\infty
, and the optimal random Gram matrix exists due to the compactness of
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{A}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices, and
p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}>0
be the weights with
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{m}=1
. Let
\hat{\mathbf{A}}\in\argmin_{\mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B})
be a weighted barycenter. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{i})\cap\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=\{0\}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{A}_{i}/\hat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{0},\quad i=1,\cdots,m.

In particular,
\rk(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\geq\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\rk(\mathbf{A}_{i})
, thus if at least one of
\mathbf{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{A}_{m}
is injective, then
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
is also injective
[
1
]
.

Proof of
Corollary

4.4
.

Without loss of generality, let
i=1
. Then,
due to
Theorem

4.3
, there exists an optimal Green matrices
\mathbf{G}_{j}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{j})
for
j=1,\cdots,m
such that
\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_{j}\mathbf{G}_{j}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
. It is trivial that
(\sum_{j=2}^{m}p_{j}\mathbf{G}_{j})^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1}\succeq\mathbf{0}
since:

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\hat{\mathbf{G}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\sum_{j=2}^{m}p_{j}\mathbf{G}_{j}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}+p_{1}^{2}\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{1})+2p_{1}\cdot\text{tr}[(\sum_{j=2}^{m}p_{j}\mathbf{G}_{j})^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1}],\quad\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{1}).

Let
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\cap\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}_{1})\cap\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*})
. Then, there exists some
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{d}
and

\displaystyle 0=\mathbf{d}^{*}\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{d}^{*}\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{d}=\mathbf{d}^{*}\left(\sum_{j=2}^{m}p_{j}\mathbf{G}_{j}\right)^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{d}+p_{1}\mathbf{c}^{*}\mathbf{c}\geq p_{1}\|\mathbf{c}\|^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{0}.

Consequently, we get
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{1})\cap\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mathbf{A}})=\{0\}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{A}_{1}/\hat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{0}
due to
Lemma

2.4
.
∎

In the meantime, proper alignment is a weaker notion than optimality, hence (
21
) cannot characterize the barycenters in general:

Example 4.1
.

Consider
\Omega=\{1,2\}
, and
\mathbb{P}({1})=1-t,\mathbb{P}({2})=t
for some
t\in(0,1)
,
and let

\mathbf{A}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&0&0\\
0&a_{2}&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{A}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&b_{2}&0\\
0&0&b_{3}\end{pmatrix},\quad a_{1},a_{2},b_{2},b_{3}>0,\quad(1-t)\sqrt{a_{2}}=\sqrt{b_{2}}.

Then, one can check that
\mathbf{G}_{1}=\mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{a_{1}},\sqrt{a_{2}},0)
and
\mathbf{G}_{2}=\mathrm{diag}(0,-\sqrt{b_{2}},\sqrt{b_{3}})
are properly aligned while
\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\succeq\mathbf{0}
is violated, thus they are not optimal. Accordingly, the induced covariance
\hat{\mathbf{A}}=\mathrm{diag}((1-t)^{2}a_{1},0,t^{2}b_{3})
, is not a barycenter while satisfying (
21
).

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{A}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices, and
p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}>0
be the weights with
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{m}=1
. If
\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{A}_{j}=\mathbf{0}
for any
1\leq i<j\leq m
, then the set of weighted Wasserstein barycenters are given by

\argmin_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B})=\left\{\hat{\mathbf{G}}\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}:\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i},\,\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{i}),\,i=1,\cdots,m\right\}.

Proof of
Corollary

4.5
.

Let
\{\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{i}):i=1,\cdots,m\}
be an arbitrary collection of Green’s matrices. Then, for any
1\leq i\neq j\leq m
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{j}\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathbf{0}\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{A}_{i}\mathbf{A}_{j}=\mathbf{0}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{j})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{i})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{j})}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{i})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}_{j})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{j}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{i}^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{i}^{*})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{G}_{j}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{i}=\mathbf{0},

i.e.,
\{\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{i}):i=1,\cdots,m\}
is optimal. Then, the results follow immediately from
Theorem

4.3
.
∎

More generally, if the covariance matrices can be grouped into several subspaces over which they act on, we can compute the barycenter by computing sub-barycenters, and we refer to
Corollary

A.5
in the appendix.

Example 4.2
.

Recall
Example

3.2
, where
\mathbf{A}=\diag(1,0)
and
\mathbf{B}=\diag(0,1)
.
Since
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{0}
, we can take any Green’s matrices to construct a weighted barycenter between
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
due to
Corollary

4.5
. We may fix
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
due to right unitary invariance. Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{B})
where

\mathbf{U}:=\begin{pmatrix}c&\pm s\\
s&\mp c\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{O}(2),\quad c^{2}+s^{2}=1.

If we denote
\mathbf{G}_{t}=(1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{t}\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}(1-t)&0\\
ts&\mp tc\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(1-t)&ts\\
0&\mp tc\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(1-t)^{2}&t(1-t)s\\
t(1-t)s&t^{2}\end{pmatrix},

hence

\displaystyle\argmin_{\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{C}\succeq\mathbf{0}}(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}(1-t)^{2}&t(1-t)s\\
t(1-t)s&t^{2}\end{pmatrix}:|s|\leq 1\right\},

which is the convex hull of two McCann’s interpolants
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{+},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{-}
at time
t\in(0,1)
in
Example

3.2
. We will see in
Section

4.3
that this is not a coincidence.

4.2
Multicoupling Problem

We generalize the multi-marginal optimal transport problem
[
41
,
1
]
into the infinite version.

Definition 4.2

(Random Kernel)
.

Let
\mathscr{A}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random covariance matrix with
\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<+\infty
. A random matrix-valued kernel
[
42
]
for
\mathscr{A}
is a measurable function
\mathscr{A}^{\times}:\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
that satisfies:

•

(Diagonal Constraint) For all
\omega\in\Omega
,
\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega)=\mathscr{A}(\omega)
.

•

(Symmetry) For all
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
,
\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega^{\prime},\omega)
.

•

(s.p.d.) For any
\omega_{1},\cdots,\omega_{m}\in\Omega
and
\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have
\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}{\left\langle{\mathbf{v}_{i}},{\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega_{i},\omega_{j})\mathbf{v}_{j}}\right\rangle}\geq 0
.

We denote the space of all such measurable kernels by
\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})
.

Note that for any
\mathscr{A}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})
, its expectation in the product measure space

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{A}^{\times}}:=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})\succeq\mathbf{0},

is well-defined and s.p.d. through limiting arguments. Additionally, observe that

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathscr{A}^{\times}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left\langle{\mathbf{e}_{k}},{\widehat{\mathscr{A}^{\times}}\mathbf{e}_{k}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\left({\left\langle{\mathbf{e}_{k}},{\mathscr{A}(\omega)\mathbf{e}_{k}}\right\rangle}{\left\langle{\mathbf{e}_{k}},{\mathscr{A}(\omega^{\prime})\mathbf{e}_{k}}\right\rangle}\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\int_{\Omega}{\left\langle{\mathbf{e}_{k}},{\mathscr{A}(\omega)\mathbf{e}_{k}}\right\rangle}^{1/2}\right)^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}.

Our goal is then to maximize its trace:
\sup\{{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathscr{A}^{\times}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}:\mathscr{A}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})\}
. From our construction, the correspondence between the barycenter problem and the multicoupling problem becomes clear.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\mathscr{A}:(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random covariance matrix with
\mathbb{E}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<+\infty
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\times}\in\argmax_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathscr{B}^{\times}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1},

if and only if the random matrix-valued kernel
\mathscr{A}^{\times}:\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})=\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathscr{G}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\quad\mathbb{P}-\text{a.s.},

for some optimal random Green’s matrix
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
. Consequently, if the optimal random Green’s matrix does not exist, neither does the barycenter. If exists,
\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathscr{G}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}
is an optimal random kernel.

Proof of
Theorem

4.6
.

For
\mathscr{A}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})
, consider the following risk:

\displaystyle L^{\times}(\mathscr{A}^{\times}):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\tr[\mathscr{A}(\omega)+\mathscr{A}(\omega^{\prime})-2\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})]\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})=-{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathscr{A}^{\times}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}+\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}\geq 0.

We claim that

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times})=\inf_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathscr{A}).

(
\geq
)

Given
\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})
, denote
\tilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\times}(\omega):=\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{B}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
\omega\in\Omega
, then the block matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{A}(\omega)&\tilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\times}(\omega)\\
[\tilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\times}(\omega)]^{*}&\widehat{\mathscr{B}}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\succeq\mathbf{0},

hence,

\displaystyle L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times})=\int_{\Omega}\tr[\mathscr{A}(\omega)-2\tilde{\mathscr{B}}^{\times}(\omega)+\widehat{\mathscr{B}}^{\times}]\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\geq\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{B}}^{\times},\mathscr{A}(\omega))\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{B}}^{\times},\mathscr{A}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times})\geq\inf_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{B}}^{\times},\mathscr{A})\geq\inf_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathscr{A}).

(
\leq
)

Let
\{\mathscr{S}_{k}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A}):k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a sequence of random Green’s matrices such that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}_{k}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=\sup_{\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}.

Accordingly, define the sequence of random kernels
\mathscr{B}_{k}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})
by
\mathscr{B}_{k}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\mathscr{S}_{k}(\omega)\mathscr{S}_{k}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}
. Then, we have
\widehat{\mathscr{B}^{\times}}=[\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}_{k}][\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}_{k}]^{*}
, hence

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times})=-\lim_{k\to\infty}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbb{E}\mathscr{S}_{k}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{E}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathscr{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}=\inf_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathscr{A}),

due to
Remark

4.1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times})\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times})=\inf_{\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{B}\succeq\mathbf{0}}\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathscr{A}).

Consider a solution to

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}^{\times}\in\argmax_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\widehat{\mathscr{B}^{\times}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}=\argmin_{\mathscr{B}^{\times}\in\mathcal{K}^{\times}(\mathscr{A})}L^{\times}(\mathscr{B}^{\times}).

Because of
Lemma

4.1
, there exists some
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
such that
\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times},\mathscr{A}(\omega))={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times})^{1/2}-\mathscr{G}(\omega)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}
for all
\omega\in\Omega
. Then,

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}L(\mathbf{G})=L^{\times}(\mathscr{A}^{\times})\geq\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times},\mathscr{A}(\omega))\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times},\mathscr{A})\geq\inf_{\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}L(\mathbf{G}),

so we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times},\mathscr{A})=\inf_{\mathbf{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}L(\mathbf{G}),

(22)

\displaystyle\tr[\mathscr{A}(\omega)-2\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\times}(\omega)+\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times}]={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times})^{1/2}-\mathscr{G}(\omega)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},\quad\mathbb{P}-\text{a.s.}.

(23)

Then, from the proof of
Theorem

4.3
, (
22
) yields that
\mathscr{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathscr{A})
is an optimal random Green’s matrix and
(\widehat{\mathscr{A}}^{\times})^{1/2}=\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G}
. Consequently, (
23
) yields that
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}^{\times}(\omega)=\mathscr{G}(\omega)(\mathbb{E}\mathscr{G})^{*}
holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
or equivalently,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{A}^{\times}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime})=\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{G}(\omega)\mathscr{G}(\omega^{\prime})^{*}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\omega^{\prime}),\quad\mathbb{P}-\text{a.s.}

∎

4.3
Kantorovich Problem

Consider
\Omega=\{0,1\}
with
\mathbb{P}({0})=1-t,\mathbb{P}({1})=t
for some
t\in[0,1]
, so the weighted barycenter is defined as

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}:=\argmin_{\mathbf{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\ \mathbf{C}\succeq\mathbf{0}}(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}).

Obviously,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}=\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}=\mathbf{B}
. Because
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}_{t}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=(1-t)^{2}\tr(\mathbf{A})+2t(1-t)\tr(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})+t^{2}\tr(\mathbf{B})
, the optimality condition for Green’s matrices reduces to:

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{A}),\ \mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B}),\ \mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0},

(24)

which does not depend on
t\in[0,1]
.
The set of weighted barycenters is determined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}):=\left\{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{G}_{t}\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}:\mathbf{G}_{t}=(1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M},\,\mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B}),\ \mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}\right\},

(25)

which is independent on the choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{A})
due to right unitary invariance, so one may fix it. When a Monge solution exists (i.e.,
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
with an OT matrix
\mathbf{T}
), setting
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
provides a valid Kantorovich solution, and the barycenter
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
in (
25
) recovers McCann’s interpolant. From
Theorem

4.6
, we note that a coupling of
N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
and
N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{B})
is optimal for the Kantorovich problem if and only if its covariance matrix has the form

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{G}\mathbf{M}^{*}\\
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{G}^{*}&\mathbf{B}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n},

(26)

where the Green’s matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a solution to (
24
).

Theorem 4.7

(Kantorovich Problem)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices with
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
. We decompose
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
in the block form as in

Theorem

3.1
, and let

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},\quad\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{11}).

Then, the set of all matrices
\mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B})
satisfying (
24
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}+\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}&\mathbf{M}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},

where:

•

\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
satisfies
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
and
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
.

•

\mathbf{M}_{22}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
satisfies
\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}
.

A solution corresponds to a Monge map (i.e.,
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
for some OT matrix
\mathbf{T}
) if and only if one chooses
\mathbf{M}_{22}=\mathbf{0}
(i.e.,
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
).

Proof of
Theorem

4.7
.

Decompose
\mathbf{M}
as:

\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}_{11}&\mathbf{M}_{12}\\
\mathbf{M}_{21}&\mathbf{M}_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
if and only if

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{M}_{11}&\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{M}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}=\begin{pmatrix}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},

which happens if and only if
\mathbf{M}_{11}=(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}
and
\mathbf{M}_{12}=\mathbf{0}
. With these values, the condition
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{B})
becomes

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{M}_{11}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}\\
\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{11}^{*}&\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}=\mathbf{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{11}&\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix},

which is equivalent to the following system of block equations:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{12},

(27)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{22}.

(28)

As in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, (
27
) always have a solution characterized by

\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})+\mathbf{N}_{12},\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}((\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}).

Substituting
\mathbf{N}_{12}
into (
28
), we obtain from part 3 in
Lemma

2.4
that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}_{22}-(\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{G}_{11})(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{12})=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\succeq\mathbf{0}.

Finally, if
\mathbf{M}_{22}=\mathbf{0}
, the above equation becomes precisely (
10
) in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. Therefore,
\mathbf{M}_{22}=\mathbf{0}
if and only if there exists some OT matrix
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The off-diagonal block,
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{M}^{*}
, of the covariance for the optimal Kantorovich coupling in (
26
) has the form

\mathbf{G}\mathbf{M}^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}&\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{B}_{12}+\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{N}_{12}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}.

This calculation reveals two key properties. First,
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{M}^{*}
is independent of the particular choice of
\mathbf{G}_{11}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{11})
and
\mathbf{M}_{22}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12})
. Second, this implies that the diagonal blocks of any barycenter
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
are fixed:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}=(1-t)^{2}\mathbf{A}_{11}+t(1-t)[\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}+\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}]+t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{11},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}=t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{22},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}=t(1-t)[\mathbf{G}_{11}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{B}_{12}+\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{N}_{12}]+t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{12},\quad(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}=(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}^{*}.

The entire geometry of the set of barycenters is determined by the choice of the submatrix
\mathbf{N}_{12}
, subject to the conditions in
Theorem

4.7
. To emphasize this dependence, we sometimes write the barycenter as
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
in the rest of this subsection.

This observation reveals the geometric structure of the set of barycenters, generalizing
Theorem

3.2
.

Theorem 4.8

(Extreme Geodesics)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices with
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
, and let
t\in(0,1)
. The set of barycenters
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
has the following geometric properties:

1.

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is an extreme point of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
if and only if it is a McCann interpolant at time
t
.

2.

\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is the closed convex hull of the set of all McCann interpolants at time
t
.

3.

For any barycenter
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}/\mathbf{A}=t^{2}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}),\quad\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}})=\rk(\mathbf{A})+\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}/\mathbf{A}).

Consequently, the following statements are equivalent:

a.

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is a McCann interpolant at time
t
.

b.

Its
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement vanishes:
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
.

c.

Its rank is preserved:
\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=\rk(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.8
.

1.

We first show that the set given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:=\left\{\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}\mid\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}),\ \mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\right\},

is a closed convex set. The closedness of
\mathcal{A}
is trivial. To show convexity, consider
\mathbf{P}_{\lambda}=(1-\lambda)\mathbf{P}_{0}+\lambda\mathbf{P}_{1}
, where
\mathbf{P}_{0},\mathbf{P}_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
. We have
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{P}_{\lambda})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{P}_{0})+\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{P}_{1})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
. Also, observe
[(1-\lambda)\mathbf{P}_{0}^{*}\mathbf{P}_{0}+\lambda\mathbf{P}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{P}_{1}]-\mathbf{P}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathbf{P}_{\lambda}=\lambda(1-\lambda)(\mathbf{P}_{0}-\mathbf{P}_{1})^{*}(\mathbf{P}_{0}-\mathbf{P}_{1})\succeq\mathbf{0}
with equality if and only if
\mathbf{P}_{0}=\mathbf{P}_{1}
, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\succeq(1-\lambda)\mathbf{P}_{0}^{*}\mathbf{P}_{0}+\lambda\mathbf{P}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{P}_{1}\succeq\mathbf{P}_{\lambda}^{*}\mathbf{P}_{\lambda}.

(29)

Thus,
\mathbf{P}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}
.

We claim that the set of extreme points
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{A}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\left\{\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}\mid\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}),\ \mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\right\}.

It is trivial that
\mathcal{E}\supseteq\text{(RHS)}
since the equality in (
29
) holds if and only if
\mathbf{P}_{0}=\mathbf{P}_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
.
To show the other direction, we may assume
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\neq\mathbf{0}
, otherwise trivial. Suppose
\mathbf{N}_{12}\in\mathcal{A}
while
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}\neq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
. Then, there exists a non-zero vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
such that
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}
.

(Case 1)

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subsetneq\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
.

Fix a unit vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})\cap\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})^{\perp}
. Define
\mathbf{R}_{12}=\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Then, we have
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{12}=\mathbf{0}
, thus

\displaystyle(\mathbf{N}_{12}\pm\mathbf{R}_{12})^{*}(\mathbf{N}_{12}\pm\mathbf{R}_{12})=\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}+\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}.

Therefore, we have
(\mathbf{N}_{12}+\mathbf{R}_{12}),(\mathbf{N}_{12}-\mathbf{R}_{12})\in\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{N}_{12}
is not an extreme point of
\mathcal{A}
.

(Case 2)

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
.

Note from
Corollary

2.5
that

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*})=\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})

\displaystyle=\rk(\mathbf{A})-\rk[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}]

\displaystyle\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})-\rk[(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}]=\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}).

Since
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})
, this entails that
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})
and the restriction of the adjoint
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}:\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})\to\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})
is invertible. Therefore, there exists some
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11})
such that
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}
. Choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
(2\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2})\leq 1
. Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{N}_{12}+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*})^{*}(\mathbf{N}_{12}+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*})=\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}+(2\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2})\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{N}_{12}-\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*})^{*}(\mathbf{N}_{12}-\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*})=\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}+(-2\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2})\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^{*}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}.

Finally, we have
(\mathbf{N}_{12}+\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*}),(\mathbf{N}_{12}-\varepsilon\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}^{*})\in\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{N}_{12}
is not an extreme point of
\mathcal{A}
.

Then, from
Remark

4.2
, it follows that
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is the closed convex set. It also follows that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is an extreme point if and only if
\mathbf{N}_{12}\in\mathcal{E}
, which is equivalent to the fact that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is a McCann interpolant at time
t
due to
Theorem

4.7
.

2.

Direct implication of the Krein-Milman theorem
[
10
]
.

The part 3 will be shown while establishing the second set of equivalence relations:

(a\Rightarrow b)

See
Theorem

3.2
.

(b\Rightarrow a)

We prove it by contradiction. Suppose
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
while
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is not a McCann interpolant. From
Theorem

4.7
, we get
\mathbf{M}_{22}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Using the block decomposition as in the proof of
Theorem

4.7
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}

\displaystyle=[(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}+t\mathbf{M}_{11}][(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}+t\mathbf{M}_{11}]^{*},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}

\displaystyle=t(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}+t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{11}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=[(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}+t\mathbf{M}_{11}][t\mathbf{M}_{21}]^{*}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{22}=t^{2}[\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}].

To ease the notation, let us denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{11}:=[(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}+t\mathbf{M}_{11}]=[(1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}]\mathbf{G}_{11},

which is injective. Then, the
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}-(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{21}\left[\mathbf{I}_{11}-\mathbf{X}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{X}_{11}\mathbf{X}_{11}^{*})^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}_{11}\right]\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{X}_{11})}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{22}=t^{2}[\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}],

hence it does not vanish, which is a contradiction.

(b\Leftrightarrow c)

For
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
,
(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}
is full-rank since
(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}\succeq(1-t)^{*}\mathbf{A}_{11}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{G})=\rk(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})=\rk((\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11})=\rk(\mathbf{G}_{11})=\rk(\mathbf{A}),

Then,
Corollary

2.5
concludes

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=\rk(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\mathbf{G})+\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A})=\rk(\mathbf{A})+\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}),

and the equivalence becomes trivial.

∎

We remark that

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{A})\leq\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})\leq\rk(\mathbf{A})+\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})=\rk(\mathbf{A})+\rk(\mathbf{B})-\rk(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}).

The geometry of the barycenter set also provides another perspective on s.p.d. reachability in
Theorem

3.3
, connecting it to the uniqueness of the Kantorovich barycenter.

Corollary 4.9

(SPD Reachability Revisited)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices. For any
t\in(0,1)
, the set of barycenters
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is a singleton if and only if the equivalent conditions in
Theorem

3.3
hold.
Furthermore, this unique barycenter is the McCann interpolant at time
t
.

Proof of
Corollary

4.9
.

Trivial from
Theorems

4.7
and
4.8
.
∎

The preceding discussion focused on the set of barycenters at a fixed time
t
. To form a geodesic path from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
, one must consistently select the free parameter
\mathbf{N}_{12}
for all
t\in(0,1)
. Each valid choice of
\mathbf{N}_{12}
in
Theorem

4.7
defines a unique geodesic. We call the collection of all such paths the
Kantorovich geodesics
.

Theorem 4.10

(Kantorovich Geodesics)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices with
\rk(\mathbf{A})\geq\rk(\mathbf{B})
.

1.

Any path
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},\,t\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
constructed by fixing a choice of
\mathbf{N}_{12}
in
Theorem

4.7
generates a constant-speed geodesic, that is,
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{s}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}})=(t-s)\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
for any
0\leq t\leq s\leq 1
.

2.

Conversely, any constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
is of this form.

3.

The rank of
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
is constant for
t\in(0,1)
and is given by
\rk(\mathbf{A})+\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12})
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.10
.

1.

Since
\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
, note that, for any
0\leq t\leq s\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left([(1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M}]^{*}[(1-s)\mathbf{G}+s\mathbf{M}]\right)

\displaystyle=(1-t)(1-s)\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{G}+ts\tr(\mathbf{B})+[(1-t)s+(1-s)t](\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})=\mathbf{G}_{s}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{t}\succeq\mathbf{0}.

By
Lemma

4.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{s})

\displaystyle=\tr(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})+\tr(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})-2\tr[(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{s}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{1/2})^{1/2}]

\displaystyle=\tr(\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{t})+\tr(\mathbf{G}_{s}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{s})-2\tr(\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{s})=\tr([\mathbf{G}_{t}-\mathbf{G}_{s}]^{*}[\mathbf{G}_{t}-\mathbf{G}_{s}])

\displaystyle=(t-s)^{2}\tr([\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{M}]^{*}[\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{M}])=(t-s)^{2}\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}).

2.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
. Fix
t\in(0,1)
and we claim that
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. For completeness, we provide the proof of the well-known sectional curvature inequality of the 2-Wasserstein distance
[
51
,
41
,
52
]
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})\geq\left(\frac{1}{1-t}+\frac{1}{t}\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}))^{2}\geq t(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}),

and the equality holds if and only if
t^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})=(1-t)^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. This shows that
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
for some
\mathbf{N}_{12}
given in
Theorem

4.7
.

It remains to show that
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
for all
t\in(0,1)
. Note that
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{B})
associated with
\mathbf{N}_{12}
satisfies
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
. Then, we obtain from
Corollary

4.4
that the reverse Schur complement vanishes:
\mathbf{A}/\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}=\mathbf{0}
. Due to
Corollary

4.9
and part 1,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}:[t,0]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
(reversed time) is the unique constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
and
\mathbf{A}
. Repeating the proof, we get that
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}:[t,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the unique constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
and
\mathbf{B}
.

3.

This follows directly from
Theorem

4.8
.

∎

Example 4.3
.

Recall s.p.d. matrices
\mathbf{A}=\diag(4,1,0)
and
\mathbf{C}=\diag(0,0,1)
in
Example

3.1
. From our earlier calculation in
Example

3.1
, all the Kantorovich geodesics connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{C}
are given by

\mathbf{\Gamma}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\,t\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}4(1-t)^{2}&0&2t(1-t)x\\
0&(1-t)^{2}&t(1-t)y\\
2t(1-t)x&t(1-t)y&t^{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1,

and they are McCann interpolants if and only if
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
. Also, as predicted by
Theorem

4.10
, we have

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=\begin{cases}2&,\quad x^{2}+y^{2}=1,\\
3&,\quad x^{2}+y^{2}<1,\end{cases}\quad t\in(0,1).

4.4
Barycenter Algorithm

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{A}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be s.p.d. matrices, and
p_{1},\cdots,p_{m}>0
be the weights with
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{m}=1
. Recall from
Theorem

4.3
that finding the barycenter amounts to solving the maximization problem:

\max\{{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\mathbf{G}}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}:\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i},\,\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{i}),\,i=1,\cdots,m\}.

We outline an efficient iterative algorithm based on block coordinate ascent to solve this problem, different to
[
6
,
43
,
35
]
. A detailed analysis of its performance is beyond the scope of this paper, and be a future topic.

In each main iteration, we cycle through
i=1,2,\cdots,m
and update each Green’s operator
\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{i})
to maximize the objective function while holding all other operators
\mathbf{G}_{j}
(for
j\neq i
) fixed. To derive the update rule for a specific
\mathbf{G}_{i}
, let
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i}:=\sum_{j\neq i}p_{j}\mathbf{G}_{j}
. The objective can then be written as:

\displaystyle{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}+\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=2p_{i}\tr[(\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i})^{*}\mathbf{G}_{i}]+p_{i}^{2}\tr(\mathbf{A}_{i})+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2},

which is maximized if and only if
\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{new}}
is the solution to

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{i}),\quad(\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i})^{*}\mathbf{G}_{i}=[(\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i})^{*}\mathbf{A}_{i}\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i}]^{1/2},

which can easily be solved either (i) using the (possibly rank-deficient) SVD decomposition of
(\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i})^{*}\mathbf{A}_{i}^{1/2}
outlined in
Algorithm

1
, or (ii) (
24
) using
Theorem

4.7
. By construction, each update increases (or leaves unchanged) the value of the objective function, ensuring that the algorithm is monotonically convergent, particularly to the global solution provided uniqueness. Also, one may incorporate a natural stopping criterion: the process can be terminated when the increase in the objective function is negligible:

{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{new}}+\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}-{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{old}}+\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}=2p_{i}\tr\left[(\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{-i})^{*}(\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{new}}-\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{old}})\right]\geq 0

Algorithm 1

Weighted Wasserstein Barycenter Algorithm

1:

2:

A set of s.p.d. matrices
\mathbf{A}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

3:

A set of weights
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}>0
with
\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}=1
.

4:

Maximum iteration counts
\text{max\_iter}\in\mathbb{N}
, and tolerance
\text{tol}>0
.

5:

6:

The weighted Wasserstein barycenter
\hat{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

7:

8:

\triangleright

— Initialization —

9:

for

i=1\to m

do

10:

Solve for
\mathbf{G}_{i}
in
\mathbf{G}_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{*}=\mathbf{A}_{i}

\triangleright
e.g., via Cholesky decomposition

11:

\hat{\mathbf{G}}\leftarrow\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}

\triangleright
Initialize the weighted average of Green’s matrices

12:

13:

\triangleright

— Main Iteration —

14:

for

k=1\to\text{max\_iter}

do

15:

for

i=1\to m

do

16:

\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{old}}\leftarrow\mathbf{G}_{i}

17:

\hat{\mathbf{G}}\leftarrow\hat{\mathbf{G}}-p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{old}}

\triangleright
Temporarily remove component
i

18:

Solve for
\mathbf{G}_{i}
in:
\mathbf{G}_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{*}=\mathbf{A}_{i}
,
\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{i}=(\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{i}\hat{\mathbf{G}})^{1/2}

\triangleright
e.g., Golub-Kahan Bidiagonalization
[
19
]

19:

\hat{\mathbf{G}}\leftarrow\hat{\mathbf{G}}+p_{i}\mathbf{G}_{i}

\triangleright
Re-incorporate the updated component

20:

if

\tr[\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}(\mathbf{G}_{i}-\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\text{old}})]<\text{tol}

then

21:

return

\hat{\mathbf{G}}\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}

\triangleright
Early stopping on convergence

22:

return

\hat{\mathbf{G}}\hat{\mathbf{G}}^{*}

\triangleright
Return result after max iterations

5
Validity of Pushforward

Henceforth, we assume
\mathcal{H}
is an infinite dimensional separable
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert space.
Extending the concept of reachability between Gaussian measures to this setting introduces two fundamental challenges: defining suitable operator domains and accommodating unbounded transport maps.

Consider a Gaussian random element
X
in
\mathcal{H}
with a covariance operator
\mathbf{A}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
. It is well established that the covariance operator of a Gaussian element must be of trace class. Conversely, for any trace-class operator
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, there exists a corresponding Gaussian measure
[
13
]
. We can therefore associate a centered Gaussian measure with its covariance operator
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. Since
\mathbf{A}
is compact, the open mapping theorem prevents it from being bijective
[
10
]
. This theorem also establishes the equivalence of the following statements:

•

\rk(\mathbf{A})<\infty
, i.e.
\mathbf{A}
has a finite-dimensional range.

•

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})
is a closed subspace in
\mathcal{H}
.

•

Equality holds in
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
.

The compactness of
\mathbf{A}
introduces a subtlety concerning the support of the associated Gaussian measure.
A key property is that
\mathbb{P}(X\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})})=1

[
24
]
. However, if
\mathbf{A}
has an infinite rank, the Karhunen-Loève expansion and the Picard criterion show that
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}))=0

[
8
,
21
]
. A classical example is Brownian motion (BM) in
\mathcal{H}=L_{2}[0,1]
, where
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
is the Cameron-Martin space (see
Example

7.1
)
[
39
,
42
,
13
]
.
This disparity raises a dilemma:
if we wish to apply an unbounded transport operator
\mathbf{T}
, its domain may not be the entire space. What, then, should its essential domain be?

A second challenge arises from the counterintuitive nature of unbounded operators in this context. Let
\{e_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\}
be an ONB for
\mathcal{H}
, and consider the covariance operators

\mathbf{A}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-a}e_{n}\otimes e_{n},\quad\mathbf{B}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-b}e_{n}\otimes e_{n},

where
a,b>1
ensures both operators are trace-class. A natural candidate for the operator that pushes forward the measure
N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
to
N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{B})
would be

\mathbf{T}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{(a-b)/2}e_{n}\otimes e_{n}.

Note that this operator is unbounded when
a>b>1
, and in fact, the series defining
\mathbf{T}
fails to converge even in the weak operator topology, meaning it is not a well-defined operator on
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}
. On the other hand, this map cannot be arbitrarily unbounded. For instance, if we attempted to pushforward to a measure where
b\leq 1
(e.g., white noise with
b=0
), the resulting measure could not exist in
\mathcal{H}
as
\mathbf{B}
would no longer be trace-class. The theory of abstract Wiener spaces demonstrates that the Hilbert space must be expanded to a larger Banach space to accommodate such measures
[
20
,
13
]
.

These challenges show that in infinite dimensions, one
cannot
simply assert that
\mathbf{T}
is optimal merely because it satisfies certain linear equations as in
Definition

3.1
. Before tackling the Monge-Kantorovich problem, we must first address a more fundamental question:
If a possibly unbounded operator
\mathbf{T}
pushes forward one Gaussian measure to another, what essential conditions must it satisfy?

5.1
Unbounded Operators

This subsection provides a brief overview of unbounded operators, which are central to our analysis. Although the theory is often developed for
\mathbb{C}
-Hilbert space to leverage their richer spectral theory
[
10
,
22
,
45
,
46
,
28
]
, our work is set in
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert spaces, which introduces certain technical nuances that we address here.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}
be Hilbert spaces.

1.

An operator
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is called
unbounded
if it is

Densely defined :
Its domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
is a dense subspace of
\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

Linearity :
For any
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{T}(\alpha f+\beta g)=\alpha\mathbf{T}(f)+\beta\mathbf{T}(g)
.

We denote the space of unbounded operators from
\mathcal{H}_{1}
to
\mathcal{H}
by
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
, or simply
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
when
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}
. An operator
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
is called
bounded
if there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\|\mathbf{T}h\|\leq c\|h\|
for all
h\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
.

2.

The
graph
of
\mathbf{T}
is the set
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{T}):=\{(h,\mathbf{T}h)\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}\mid h\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})\}
.
The operator
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
is called
closed
if its graph
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{T})
is closed in
\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}
. We denote the space of closed operators by
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
, or simply
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
when
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{H}
.

3.

For
\mathbf{T},\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
, we say that
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
is an
extension
of
\mathbf{T}
, denoted by
\mathbf{T}\subset\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
, if
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{T})\subset\mathcal{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{T}})
.

4.

An operator
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
is called
closable
if it admits a closed extension
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
. Its extension
\bar{\mathbf{T}}
is called the
closure
if
\mathcal{G}(\bar{\mathbf{T}})=\overline{\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{T})}
.

Note that
unbounded
does not necessarily mean
not bounded
. However, a bounded operator has only a trivial bounded extension, making this case uninteresting. Additionally, a closed operator is defined everywhere if and only if it is bounded by the closed graph theorem
[
10
]
.

Definition 5.2

(Adjoint)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
. The domain of its adjoint is defined as

\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*}):=\{h\in\mathcal{H}:g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})\mapsto{\left\langle{h},{\mathbf{T}g}\right\rangle}\text{ is a bounded linear functional}\}.

For each
h\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*})
, the Riesz representation theorem guarantees the existence of a unique vector
\mathbf{T}^{*}h\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
such that
{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}^{*}h},{g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{h},{\mathbf{T}g}\right\rangle}
for all
g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
. This defines the
adjoint
of
\mathbf{T}
as the linear operator
\mathbf{T}^{*}:\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*})\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

The adjoint has several key properties. It is always a closed operator, thus
\mathbf{T}^{*}
is defined everywhere if and only if
\mathbf{T}
is bounded. Furthermore, if
\mathbf{A}\subset\mathbf{B}
, then
\mathbf{B}^{*}\subset\mathbf{A}^{*}
. A fundamental property is the
duality
:

{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}^{*}h},{g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{h},{\mathbf{T}g}\right\rangle},\quad h\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*}),\ g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}).

(30)

Additionally,
\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathcal{H}
is the graph of an operator if and only if it is a linear subspace with the single valued property:
(0,g)\in\mathcal{G}\,\Rightarrow\,g=0
, from which the following equivalence follows:

•

\mathbf{T}
is closable.

•

If
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})\to 0
and
\mathbf{T}f_{n}\to g
, then
g=0
.

•

There exists a closed extension of
\mathbf{T}
.

•

\mathbf{T}^{*}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
is densely defined.

In this case, we have
\bar{\mathbf{T}}=\mathbf{T}^{**}
, which is the minimal closed extension. Consequently,
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
if and only if
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}^{**}
.

5.2
Admissible Operators

Let
X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
be a Gaussian random element with covariance operator
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. For the expression
\mathbf{T}X
to be well-defined, the domain of
\mathbf{T}
(or a suitable extension) must contain a set of full measure for the law of
X
. Since
\mathbb{P}(X\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}}))=1
, we naturally restrict our operators to have domains within this subspace. The following lemma establishes that any such subspace of full measure must be dense.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
. Then for any linear subspace
\mathcal{D}\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}})
, we have
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{D})=1
only if
\mathcal{D}
is dense in
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}})
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.1
.

Assume that
\mathcal{D}
is not dense in
\mathcal{H}
. Then, there exists a nonzero element
h\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}})\cap\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
. By the definition of Gaussianity
[
24
,
13
]
, we have
{\left\langle{h},{X}\right\rangle}\sim N(0,{\left\langle{h},{\mathbf{A}h}\right\rangle})

with non-zero variance due to the injectivity of
\mathbf{A}
on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. However, we also have

\displaystyle 1=\mathbb{P}({\left\langle{h},{X}\right\rangle}\neq 0)=\mathbb{P}({\left\langle{h},{X}\right\rangle}\neq 0,X\in\mathcal{D})=0,

which is a contradiction. Hence, it must be that
\overline{\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{H}_{1}
.
∎

In light of this lemma, we henceforth consider operators
\mathbf{T}
whose domains are dense subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}
.
A key challenge is that the measurability of

\mathbf{T}X
is not guaranteed, as an operator must typically be continuous to be Borel measurable
[
29
, Theorem. 9.10]
. The next theorem provides sufficient conditions for a closable operator

\mathbf{T}
to admit an extension

\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
for which

\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X
is a well-defined random element.

Theorem 5.2

(Pre-pushforward)
.

Let
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}}),\mathcal{H})
be an unbounded operator, and let
X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. Assume that
\mathbf{T}
is closable and
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. If
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
is any extension of the closure
\overline{\mathbf{T}}
of
\mathbf{T}
, then
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}))=1
and

\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}).

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

Consider the spectral decomposition of the covariance
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
:

\mathbf{A}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}e_{k}\otimes e_{k},

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots>0
are positively-valued real sequence that is non-increasing and
\{e_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an ONB of
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}
. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\xi_{k}:={\left\langle{X},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}/\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}
so that
\xi_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}N(0,1)
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle X_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\xi_{k}e_{k}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}),\quad S_{n}:=\mathbf{T}X_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})e_{k}.

(Step 1)

We claim that
S_{n}
is a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Cauchy sequence. As in
[
26
, Lemma 3.15]
, we get for any
\varepsilon>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\sup_{k\geq n}\|S_{k}-S_{n}\|>\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k\geq n}\mathbb{E}\|\xi_{k}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})e_{k}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k\geq n}\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})e_{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})e_{k}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}<\infty,

and thus
\sup_{k\geq n}\|S_{k}-S_{n}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0
as
n\to\infty
. Hence, there is a subsequence
\{n^{\prime}\}\subset\{n\}
such that
\sup_{k\geq n^{\prime}}\|S_{k}-S_{n^{\prime}}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}0
as
n^{\prime}\to\infty
. Because
\sup_{k\geq n}\|S_{k}-S_{n}\|
is a non-increasing sequence, this entails that
\sup_{k\geq n}\|S_{k}-S_{n}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}0
, i.e.
S_{n}
is a.s. Cauchy convergent, hence there is a random element
S
in
\mathcal{H}
such that
S_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}S
.

(Step 2)

Plugging in
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{I}
in (Step 1), or using the white noise expansion theorem
[
13
]
, we also obtain that
X_{n}
is a.s. Cauchy convergent. Then, Bessel’s equality concludes that
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}X
.

(Step 3)

Consider a Borel measurable set
\Omega_{X}
on which both
X_{n}\to X
and
S_{n}\to S
hold. Note that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{X})=1
. Consider any
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\supset\mathbf{T}^{**}=\overline{\mathbf{T}}
. Then, on
\Omega_{X}
, we get

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X_{n}=\overline{\mathbf{T}}X_{n}=\mathbf{T}X_{n}=S_{n}\to S,\quad X_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})\subset\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathbf{T}})\to X.

Since
\overline{\mathbf{T}}
is a closed operator, this yields that
X\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathbf{T}})
and
S=\overline{\mathbf{T}}X=\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X
on
\Omega_{X}
, i.e.
S_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{\to}}\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X
. Finally, because
S_{n}
’s are Gaussian and
\Cov[S_{n}]\to(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}
, we conclude that
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*})
.

∎

Theorem

5.2
shows that if a closable operator
\mathbf{T}
is sufficiently regular – in the sense that
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
– then it can be extended to define a valid pushforward measure, e.g.,
\overline{\mathbf{T}}
. Furthermore, the theorem reveals a notable infinite-dimensional phenomenon: the resulting covariance operator is determined entirely by how the
pre-pushforward

\mathbf{T}

acts over
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, which is a
N_{\mathcal{H}}(0,\mathbf{A})
-null set.

Remark 5.1
.

The Hilbert-Schmidt condition does not imply the closability condition. To see this, consider an ONB
\{e_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{H}
, and define

\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{-2n}e_{n}\otimes e_{n},\quad\mathbf{T}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{n}e_{1}\otimes e_{n}.

Then,
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
but
\mathbf{T}
fails to be closable:
2^{-n}e_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})\to 0
but
\mathbf{T}(2^{-n}e_{n})\rightarrow e_{1}\neq 0
.

We also note that if
\mathbf{T}
is closable, then its restriction
\mathbf{T}\mid_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
as well. We may therefore assume the domain of pre-pushforward to be
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
. The next theorem provides a converse to
Theorem

5.2
: for a closable pushforward operator, the Hilbert-Schmidt condition is not only sufficient but also necessary:

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be closable, and let
X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
. If
\mathbf{T}X
is a random element in
\mathcal{H}
, then it must be Gaussian and
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.
Specifically,

\mathbf{T}X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}).

Proof of
Theorem

5.3
.

Choose any
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}^{*})=\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*})
. Then for any
g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, or equivalently,
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
, we have from (
30
) that

\displaystyle{\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{T}^{*}f},{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}^{*}f},{g}\right\rangle},

which shows that
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}^{*}\subset(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}))=1
, note that, for any
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*})
,

\displaystyle{\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{T}X}\right\rangle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}-a.s.}}{{\equiv}}{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}^{*}f},{X}\right\rangle}\sim N(0,{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}^{*}f},{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}f}\right\rangle}),

with
{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}^{*}f},{\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}f}\right\rangle}=\|\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}^{*}f\|^{2}=\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f\|^{2}
.

Now, let
f\in\mathcal{H}
. Since
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
, there exists a sequence
f_{n}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}^{*})
such that
f_{n}\to f
in
\mathcal{H}
. For any fixed
\varepsilon>0
, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|{\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{T}X}\right\rangle}-{\left\langle{f_{n}},{\mathbf{T}X}\right\rangle}|>\varepsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\|\mathbf{T}X\|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\|f_{n}-f\|}\right).

Because any probability measure on
\mathcal{H}
is tight
[
7
]
, the right-hand side vanishes as
n\to\infty
, implying convergence in probability:

{\left\langle{f_{n}},{\mathbf{T}X}\right\rangle}\sim N(0,\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f_{n}\|^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\rightarrow}}{\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{T}X}\right\rangle}.

(31)

This results in the fact that
\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f_{n}\|
converges to some constant
\sigma_{f}\geq 0
, and
{\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{T}X}\right\rangle}\sim N(0,\sigma_{f}^{2})
.

Applying the Banach-Alaoglu theorem
[
10
]
to the bounded sequence
\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f_{n}\|
, there is a subsequence
(n^{\prime})\subset(n)
such that
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f_{n^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle wk}}{{\rightarrow}}h
with some
h\in\mathcal{H}
. For any
g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
,

\displaystyle{\left\langle{g},{h}\right\rangle}=\lim_{n^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty}{\left\langle{g},{(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f_{n^{\prime}}}\right\rangle}=\lim_{n^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty}{\left\langle{(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})g},{f_{n^{\prime}}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})g},{f}\right\rangle},

which demonstrates that
f\in\mathcal{D}[(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}]
and
h=(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}f
. However, since
f\in\mathcal{H}
was chosen arbitrary, this implies that
\mathcal{D}[(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}]=\mathcal{H}
, i.e.
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
due to the Closed Graph Theorem, thus
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
. Therefore,
\mathbf{T}X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*})
, and since its covariance should be of trace-class
[
13
]
, we obtain
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.
∎

As a direct application,
Theorems

5.2
and
5.3
yield the Driscoll 0-1 law
[
16
,
34
]
:

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. For
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
,
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}))=1
if and only if
\mathbf{A}^{1/2-\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}
and define
\mathbf{T}:=(\mathbf{A}^{\alpha})^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{\alpha})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}
. Then
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})
is closed, so the result follows immediately from
Theorems

5.2
and
5.3
since
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2-\alpha}
.
∎

Finally, we consider the case where the closability condition on the pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}
is dropped. The following theorem shows that an extension is still possible, although it is less explicit than the closure:

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be an unbounded operator, and let
X\sim N_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
where
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. If
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
, then there exists an extension
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{H}
of
\mathbf{T}
such that
\mathcal{V}
has full
N_{\mathcal{H}}(0,\mathbf{A})
-measure. In this case,

(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}X,X)\sim N_{\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{0},(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2},\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2},\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}).

Proof of
Theorem

5.5
.

We follow the proof similar to that of
[
21
, Theorem 4.47]
. Noting that the RKHS associated with
N_{\mathcal{H}}(0,\mathbf{A})
is given by (
1
),
it suffices to show that
\mathbf{T}:\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, . Consider the spectral decomposition of
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
as in the proof of
Theorem

5.2
. Then
\{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an ONB of
\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}},{\mathbf{T}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}e_{k}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k}},{\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}<\infty,

which shows that
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A}),\mathcal{H}):\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the linear operator of finite rank:

\mathbf{S}_{n}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H},\,h\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left\langle{h},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{T}e_{k},e_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left\langle{h},{\frac{e_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k},\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k}).

Define the linear subspace

\mathcal{V}:=\{h\in\mathcal{H}:\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{S}_{n}(h)\text{ exists in }\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}\},

and let
\mathbf{S}:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
by
\mathbf{S}(h)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{S}_{n}(h)
for
h\in\mathcal{V}
. To see that
\mathbf{S}
is an extension of
(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{I})
, observe that for any
h=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g\in

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{n}h

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g},{\frac{e_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k},\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left\langle{g},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k},\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k})=(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2},\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\left\langle{g},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}e_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\to(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g,\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g)=(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{I})h

as
n\to\infty
by the Plancherel equality.
Note that
\mathbf{S}_{n}(X)
is an
(\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H})
-valued martingale with

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{S}_{n}(X)\|_{\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k},\mathbf{A}^{1/2}e_{k})\|_{\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2},\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{A}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}<\infty,

hence
\mathcal{V}
has a full
N_{\mathcal{H}}(0,\mathbf{A})
-measure by the Doob martingale convergence theorem
[
26
]
. Using characteristic function as in
[
21
, Proposition 4.40]
,
\mathbf{S}X
is a Gaussian random element, with the covariance given by
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2},\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2},\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H})
, and letting
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}
to be the projection of
\mathbf{S}
onto
\mathcal{V}
completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The proof of
Theorem

5.5
shows that
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
if and only if
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A}),\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathbb{H}(\mathbf{A})
is the RKHS associated with
N_{\mathcal{H}}(0,\mathbf{A})
. This perspective reinforces a key insight from the previous theorems: the resulting pushforward measure is determined solely by the action of the pre-pushforward operator
\mathbf{T}
on the RKHS.

6
Hilbertian Reachability

Definition 6.1

(Reachability in Infinite Dimension)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, and
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators, and let
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}
. We say that
\mathbf{B}
is
unboundedly reachable
from
\mathbf{A}
, denoted
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
, if there exists an unbounded operator
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}=\mathbf{B},

(32)

\displaystyle\tr(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})=\tr(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A})=\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

(33)

Such an operator
\mathbf{T}
called an
optimal pre-pushforward
.
If
\mathbf{T}
can be chosen to be bounded, then we say that
\mathbf{B}
is
boundedly reachable
from
\mathbf{A}
, denoted
\mathbf{A}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
.

(
32
) automatically guarantees
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. As established in
Section

5
,
\mathbf{T}
itself is a pre-pushforward that admits a valid pushforward extension, and the resulting Gaussian measure depends only on
\mathbf{T}
. If
\mathbf{T}
is not bounded, then
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{T}^{*}\subsetneq(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}=\mathbf{B}
, since (LHS) is not defined everywhere. Indeed, (RHS) is a trivial extension of (LHS).

Remark 6.1
.

As in the Euclidean case, we may replace
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
by
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
for any Green’s operator
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
in (
32
) and (
33
):

Transportability :

(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})^{*}=\mathbf{B}
,

Optimality :

\tr(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})=\tr(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A})=\tr[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}]
,

due to the polar decomposition
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
with
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}
, leading to
(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})=(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\mathbf{U}
and
(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}=\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
.
As shown in
Lemma

A.1
, the optimality condition is equivalent to requiring either
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}
or
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
.

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
. Then
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
if and only if there is some
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
satisfying any of the following equivalent sets of conditions:

•

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M})
,
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}=\mathbf{B}
, and
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}
.

•

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{M})
,
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{*}=\mathbf{B}
, and
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
.

These conditions are independent of the choice of Green’s operator
\mathbf{G}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Trivial by considering
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\mathbf{G}h\mapsto\mathbf{M}h
.
∎

As in the finite dimensional case, we investigate the following question:
How can reachability be characterized, and can an optimal pre-pushforward be extended to be symmetric or even s.p.d.?

Definition 6.2
.

Let
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
be an unbounded operator. Then,

1.

\mathbf{T}
is called
symmetric
if
{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}f},{g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{T}g}\right\rangle}
for any
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
, i.e.
\mathbf{T}\subset\mathbf{T}^{*}
.

2.

\mathbf{T}
is called
self-adjoint
if
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}^{*}
.

3.

\mathbf{T}
is called
semi-positive definite
(s.p.d.) if it is symmetric and
{\left\langle{\mathbf{T}f},{f}\right\rangle}\geq 0
for any
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{T})
.

By definition, any symmetric operator is closable, and a self-adjoint operator admits no symmetric extension other than itself.
Any self-adjoint operator is symmetric and closed. However, in contrast to the bounded case, a closed symmetric operator is not necessarily self-adjoint
[
10
, Example X.1.12]
or
[
22
, Section 9.6]
. Additionally, given
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, we remark that
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
is self-adjoint. If
\mathbf{A}
is injective, then
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}
is boundedly invertible on
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})
, with its unique right inverse
\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
with
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{C})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
. Define the operator
\mathbf{S}:=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}:\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
. Then the following holds:

1.

\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
is an s.p.d. operator.

2.

If we let
\mathbf{R}:=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, then
\mathbf{R}^{*}=\overline{\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{R}^{*}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
.

3.

\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
is closed and
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}:=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\overline{\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}}
is a self-adjoint extension of
\mathbf{S}
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.2
.

Note that
\mathbf{R}:=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
is a bounded operator by the Douglas lemma.

1.

We remark
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{S})=\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.
Also, for any
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})
, let
k:=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}f,h:=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}g\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})
. Then, it holds that
f-\mathbf{A}^{1/2}k\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, thus

\mathbf{S}f=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}k=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}k=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}k,

and similarly,
\mathbf{S}g=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}h
. Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle{\left\langle{\mathbf{S}f},{g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}k},{g}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{C}k},{\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}g}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle={\left\langle{\mathbf{C}k},{h}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle={\left\langle{k},{\mathbf{C}h}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}f},{\mathbf{C}h}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}h}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{f},{\mathbf{S}g}\right\rangle},

which establishes that
\mathbf{S}
is is a s.p.d. operator.

2.

By
Lemma

A.4
,
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}=(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
is defined everywhere, hence
\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}
is closable. Therefore,
\mathbf{R}^{*}=(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})^{**}=\overline{\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}}
. Applying
Lemma

A.4
again, we also get

\mathbf{S}^{*}=(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})^{*}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{R}^{*}.

Also, since
\mathbf{S}
is symmetric,
\mathbf{S}^{*}
is densely defined in
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{S}^{*}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
.

3.

Note that
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{C}^{1/2})\oplus\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{C}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}^{1/2})
are dense in
\mathcal{H}
, thus it is immediate from
Lemma

A.4
that
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}^{1/2}=(\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2})^{*}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H})
. This yields that
\mathbf{Q}:=\mathbf{C}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}
is closable. Define
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}:=\mathbf{Q}^{*}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
so that
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{Q}^{*}\mathbf{Q}\subset\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
. The closure of symmetric operator is symmetric
[
10
]
, and one can easily verify that
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is also s.p.d..

We claim that
\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is boundedly invertible.
Observe that
\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is injective since
\mathbf{Q}^{*}=\mathbf{Q}^{***}=\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}
and

{\left\langle{(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})h},{h}\right\rangle}=\|h\|^{2}+\|\overline{\mathbf{Q}}h\|^{2}\geq\|h\|^{2},\quad h\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{\mathbf{S}}).

Note that
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{G}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}})\oplus\mathcal{J}[\mathcal{G}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{*})]

[
10
]
