. Then, for any
f\in\mathcal{H}
, there exists
x\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}})
and
y\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{*})
such that

(f,0)=(x,\overline{\mathbf{Q}}x)\oplus(-\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}y,y),

which leads to

f=x-\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}y=x+\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}\overline{\mathbf{Q}}x=(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})x.

This demonstrates that
\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is bijective, and

\|(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})h\|^{2}=\|h\|^{2}+2\|\overline{\mathbf{Q}}h\|^{2}+\|\tilde{\mathbf{S}}h\|^{2}\geq\|h\|^{2},\quad h\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{\mathbf{S}}),

shows that
{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})^{-1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\infty}\leq 1
.
Since
\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is boundedly invertible, it is closed
[
10
]
. If
(h_{n},\tilde{\mathbf{S}}h_{n})\in\mathcal{G}(\tilde{\mathbf{S}})\to(f,g)\in\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
, then

f=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n},\quad f+g=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})h_{n},

hence
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})=\mathcal{D}(\tilde{\mathbf{S}})
and
f+g=(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})f
, i.e.
g=\tilde{\mathbf{S}}f
. This shows that
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is closed.

Now, suppose
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is not self-adjoint, i.e.
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}\subsetneq\tilde{\mathbf{S}}^{*}
. Note that
(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})^{*}=\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}}^{*}
since
\mathbf{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, so we get
(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})\subsetneq(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})^{*}
. Meanwhile,
\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is surjective from the claim, meaning that
\mathcal{N}[(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})^{*}]\neq\{0\}
. However,

\mathcal{N}[(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})^{*}]=\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{I}+\tilde{\mathbf{S}})]^{\perp}=\{0\},

which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is self-adjoint.

∎

We remark that
\tilde{\mathbf{S}}
is not necessarily the unique self-adjoint extension of
\mathbf{S}
. For further details, see discussions on essential self-adjointness and maximal symmetric extensions
[
22
,
10
]
.

6.1
Injective Case

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators with
\mathbf{A}\succ\mathbf{0}
, and let
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
be any Green’s operator for
\mathbf{A}
. Define

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}}:=(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}.

Then
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
, and the following holds:

1.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}}
is independent of the choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

2.

\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}}
is s.p.d. and is the unique optimal pre-pushforward.

3.

(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/4}\overline{(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/4}\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}}
is a self-adjoint extension of
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}}
and thus a valid pushforward.

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

In the proof, we denote
\mathbf{S}
instead of
\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}}
to ease notation. By the Douglas lemma,
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}
is bounded, hence
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})=\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{S})
.

1.

Consider the polar decomposition
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
, where
\mathbf{U}
is a unique partial isometry with initial space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}^{*})}
and final space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G})}=\mathcal{H}
since
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\{0\}
. Then,
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{I}
, and we get

(\mathbf{G}^{*})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2},

which does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{G}
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
. From
Proposition

6.2
,
\mathbf{S}
is s.p.d.. Note that when
\mathbf{A}\succ\mathbf{0}
, we have
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{I}
. Also, by the polar decomposition, there exists a partial isometry
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
such that
(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}
. This implies that
\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}=[\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}]\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. Then
\mathbf{S}
satisfies (
32
) and (
33
), so it is optimal:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{*}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=\mathbf{B},

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=[\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}](\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}=(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}.

To show the uniqueness, let
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be an optimal pre-pushforward. Then, (
32
) implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}=(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*},

hence (
33
) and
Lemma

A.1
yield
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
. Since
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\succ\mathbf{0}
, we get
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{T}
.

3.

Straightforward from
Propositions

6.2
and
5.2
.

∎

Remark 6.2
.

Even with a suitable choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
, computing the square root
(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}
could still be demanding. However, if one can show that a candidate operator
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
satisfies (
32
) and
(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})\succeq\mathbf{0}
, i.e.

{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}h},{(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})h}\right\rangle}\geq 0,\quad h\in\mathcal{H},

then this is sufficient to establish the optimality of
\mathbf{T}
without explicitly computing the square root. See
Section

7
for examples.

Assuming
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B})
is effectively equivalent to assuming
\mathbf{A}
is injective (
\mathbf{A}\succ\mathbf{0}
), as we can restrict the analysis to the subspace
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A})}
without loss of generality. In this setting, bounded reachability can be characterized as follows:

Theorem 6.4

(Bounded Reachability)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators with
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B})
. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
.

2.

There exists a constant
c\geq 0
such that
(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\preceq c\mathbf{A}
.

3.

There exists some
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
such that
(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, these conditions are met.

Proof of
Theorem

6.4
.

(
1\Rightarrow 3)

Trivial.

(
3\Rightarrow 1)

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{T}}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{T}
, which trivially satisfies (
33
) as
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
. On the other hand, we have
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}-(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}]\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\mathbf{0}
,
hence (
32
) is also satisfied:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}=(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\tilde{\mathbf{T}}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}.

(
1\Rightarrow 2)

Since any bounded operator is closable with a trivial extension, note that
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
if and only if there exists a
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
such that (
32
) and (
33
) hold. Let
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{T}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}=c\geq 0
. Then, for any
h\in\mathcal{H}
,

{\left\langle{h},{(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}h}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{h},{(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})h}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}h},{\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}h}\right\rangle}\leq c\|\mathbf{A}^{1/2}h\|^{2}=c{\left\langle{h},{\mathbf{A}h}\right\rangle}.

(
2\Rightarrow 1)

By the Douglas lemma,
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/4}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
. Also, since
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{N}[(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}]
,

\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\mathbf{A}^{1/2}h\rightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}h,

or equivalently,
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}
is a well-defined operator. Note that
\mathbf{T}
is bounded with its closure:

\overline{\mathbf{T}}=[\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/4}][\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/4}]^{*}.

Also, since
\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\overline{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}
, we
\mathbf{T}

satisfies (
32
):

\displaystyle(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{*}=[\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}]\mathbf{B}[\overline{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}}]=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}=\mathbf{B},

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{1/2}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}=(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}.

Finally, if
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, there exists some
c\geq 0
such that
\mathbf{B}\preceq c\mathbf{A}
. Then, we get from the Löwner-Heinz inequality
[
33
,
27
]
that

\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\preceq c^{2}\mathbf{A}^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}\preceq(c^{2}\mathbf{A}^{2})^{1/2}=c\mathbf{A}.

∎

6.2
Non-Injective Case

We now return to the general case and represent the pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}
in block operator form:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{T}_{11}\\
\mathbf{T}_{21}\end{pmatrix}\ :\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},\text{ where }\mathbf{T}_{i1}:=\mathbf{\Pi}_{i}\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{i},\quad i=1,2.

To analyze reachability in this setting, we must account for a known subtlety of compact operators in infinite dimensions.
For compact operators
\mathbf{T}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
, while the Riesz-Schauder theorem ensures that
\rk(\mathbf{T})=\rk(\mathbf{T}^{*})
, it is possible that
\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{T})\neq\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{T}^{*})
, a phenomenon studied via the Fredholm index
[
10
]
. For instance, consider an ONB
\{e_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{H}
, and define
\mathbf{T}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-2025}e_{n}\otimes e_{2n}
. Then
\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{T})=0
, whereas
\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{T}^{*})=\infty
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators.
Then
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
if and only if the dimensional inequality holds:

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}]\leq\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}].

If it does, any optimal pre-pushforward has the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\\
[(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}+\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]^{*}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},

where
\mathbf{U}_{12}
is a partial isometry with initial space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
, and final space contained in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

(
\Rightarrow
)

Choose an optimal pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
. Note that (
32
) amounts to the fact that
\mathbf{P}:=(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{\dagger}\mathbf{B}^{1/2}
is a bounded operator satisfying
\mathbf{B}^{1/2}=(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\mathbf{P}
. Also, from (
33
), we have
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}
, which is a symmetric bounded operator.

To prove the claim, it suffices to show that

\mathbf{P}\mathbf{B}^{\dagger/2}\mid_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}.

is a well-defined injective operator. For well-definedness, we claim that
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{B}^{\dagger/2}y\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
for any
y\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}
. By letting
z=\mathbf{B}^{\dagger/2}y
so that

\displaystyle y=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}z=(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\mathbf{P}z\in\mathcal{H}_{2},

(37)

it is equivalent to showing that
\mathbf{P}z=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{B}^{\dagger/2}y\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
. For any
x\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,

\displaystyle 0={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}x},{y}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}^{1/2}x},{(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\mathbf{P}z}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{x},{(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\mathbf{P}z}\right\rangle},

which establishes
(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\mathbf{P}z=0
, thus

\mathbf{P}z\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}[(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}]=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}z\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{\dagger}]=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}_{1}.

Finally, the injectivity of
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{B}^{\dagger/2}:y\mapsto\mathbf{P}z
is immediate from (
37
):
\mathbf{P}z=0
if and only if
y=0
.

(
\Leftarrow
)

Let
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, and define
\mathbf{M}_{i1}:=\mathbf{T}_{i1}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Then (
32
) becomes:

\mathbf{M}_{i1}\mathbf{M}_{j1}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{ij},\quad i,j=1,2,

(38)

and (
33
) becomes:

\tr[\mathbf{A}_{11}\mathbf{M}_{11}]=\tr[(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

These two conditions determine
\mathbf{T}_{11}
and
\mathbf{M}_{11}
uniquely:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{11}=\mathbf{S}_{\mathbf{A}_{11}\leadsto\mathbf{B}_{11}}=\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{11}=\mathbf{T}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}=\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}.

Hence, it suffices to show that there exists
\mathbf{M}_{21}
satisfying (
38
) for
(i,j)=(1,2),(2,2)
(as the case
(2,1)
is redundant):

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1},

(39)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{22}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}.

(40)

Consider a s.p.d. block operator defined by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{C}_{11}&\mathbf{C}_{12}\\
\mathbf{C}_{21}&\mathbf{C}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}&\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}\\
\mathbf{B}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}&\mathbf{B}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}
, (
39
) is equivalent to
\mathbf{C}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{C}_{12}
, which always have a solution characterized by

\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{C}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}_{12}\oplus\mathbf{N}_{12},\quad\mathbf{N}_{12}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1}),\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{C}_{11}).

Applying this form into (
40
), we obtain from
Lemma

2.4
that
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{C}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
.
Therefore, it suffices to prove that there is some
\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
and
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
. Given that

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})=\rk[(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]

\displaystyle=\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}]=\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}),

due to
Lemma

2.4
, there is a partial isometry
\mathbf{U}_{12}
with initial space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
, and final space included in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
. Then,
\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}
is a desired solution, and any such solution takes this form.

∎

Remark 6.3
.

When
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the dimensional inequality reduces to:

\displaystyle\rk(\mathbf{B})-\rk(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})

\displaystyle=\rk(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})=\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}]=\rk(\mathbf{A})-\rk(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}),

so we recover the condition in
Theorem

3.1
:
\rk(\mathbf{B})\leq\rk(\mathbf{A})
.

The following corollary reveals that the Kantorovich problem can always be reduced to the Monge problem, either from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
or vice versa.

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators.
Then, either
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
or
\mathbf{B}\leadsto\mathbf{A}
holds.

Proof of
Corollary

6.6
.

Suppose neither
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
or
\mathbf{B}\leadsto\mathbf{A}
holds. From
Theorem

6.5
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})]>\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})}],

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})]>\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}].

We claim that

\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\mapsto\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}f\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})},

is a well-defined injective operator. For any
f\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})
, there exists a unique
g=\mathbf{A}^{\dagger/2}f\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})}
such that
f=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g=\mathbf{B}^{1/2}f=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad g\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}).

The injectivity is straightforward since
g=0
implies
f=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}g=0
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})}]\geq\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})].

and similarly,

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}]\geq\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})].

However, this results in

\displaystyle\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})]

\displaystyle>\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})}]\geq\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})]

\displaystyle>\dim[\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})}]\geq\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})],

which is a contradiction.
∎

Unlike the finite-dimensional case, constructing a symmetric extension of an optimal pre-pushforward is challenging. For such an extension to exist,
\mathbf{T}
must be symmetric, which requires the operator block
\mathbf{T}_{21}
in (
6.5
) to be closable. This closability condition is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}h_{n}\to 0,\quad[(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}+\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]^{*}h_{n}\to f\quad\Rightarrow\quad f=0.

It is trivial that any s.p.d. pushforward, if one exists, is optimal. The following theorem reveals that a necessary condition for the existence of an s.p.d. pushforward is the uniqueness of the optimal pre-pushforward:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators. The following statements are equivalent:

1.

There exists a unique optimal pre-pushforward from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{T}_{11}\\
\mathbf{T}_{21}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\\
((\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})^{*}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\end{pmatrix}\ :\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}.

2.

\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
.

3.

\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}^{1/2})
.

4.

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A})=\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

If an s.p.d. extension of an optimal pre-pushforward exists, then any of these equivalent conditions hold and
\mathbf{T}_{21}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
from (
1
) is closable.

Proof of
Theorem

6.7
.

(
1\Leftrightarrow 2)

Trivial since
\dim[\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}]]=\dim[\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{H}_{2}]
due to
Lemma

2.4
, and
-\mathbf{U}_{12}
in
Theorem

6.5
is also a partial isometry with initial space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
, and final space included in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
.

(
3\Leftrightarrow 4)

Trivial by definition.

(
2\Leftrightarrow 4)

Direct consequence of part 4 in
Lemma

2.4
.

We now claim that an s.p.d. extension of an optimal pre-pushforward is possible only if
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{T}_{21}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
in (
1
) is closable.
Let
\mathbf{S}:\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{S})\subset\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
an s.p.d. pushforward. Assume that
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\succeq\mathbf{0}
does not vanish. We may restrict
\mathbf{S}
so that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{S})\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})
, which is still an s.p.d. operator. Note that
\mathbf{S}_{12}\subset\mathbf{S}_{21}^{*}
due to symmetry, and

\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{S}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\succeq\mathbf{0}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{S}_{11}=\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2},

which shows that
\mathbf{S}
is an extension of an optimal pre-pushforward. Thus, due to
Theorem

6.5
, there exists a partial isometry
\mathbf{U}_{12}
with initial space
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})}
, and final space included in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
such that

(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{*}-(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}=\mathbf{U}_{12}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A})^{1/2}.

(50)

We now claim that
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{12}^{*})
. Since
\mathbf{S}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{H})
is densely defined, we have
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{S}_{12})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{12}^{*})
, thus it suffices to show that
{\left\langle{h_{1}},{\mathbf{S}_{12}g_{2}}\right\rangle}=0
for any
h_{1}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11})
and
g_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{S}_{12})
. For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
h_{1}\oplus\alpha g_{2}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{S})
, thus

\displaystyle 0\leq{\left\langle{(h_{1}\oplus\alpha g_{2})},{\alpha\mathbf{S}(h_{1}\oplus\alpha g_{2})}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle={\left\langle{h_{1}\oplus\alpha g_{2}},{\alpha\mathbf{S}_{12}g_{2}\oplus(\mathbf{S}_{21}h_{1}+\alpha\mathbf{S}_{22}g_{2})}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\alpha{\left\langle{h_{1}},{\mathbf{S}_{12}g_{2}}\right\rangle}+\alpha{\left\langle{g_{2}},{\mathbf{S}_{21}h_{1}}\right\rangle}+\alpha^{2}{\left\langle{g_{2}},{\mathbf{S}_{22}g_{2}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=2\alpha{\left\langle{h_{1}},{\mathbf{S}_{12}g_{2}}\right\rangle}+\alpha^{2}{\left\langle{g_{2}},{\mathbf{S}_{22}g_{2}}\right\rangle},

where we used

\displaystyle{\left\langle{g_{2}},{\mathbf{S}_{21}h_{1}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{g_{2}},{\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}h_{1}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}g_{2}},{\mathbf{S}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}h_{1}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{S}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}g_{2}},{\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}h_{1}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{\Pi}_{2}g_{2}},{h_{1}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{S}_{12}g_{2}},{h_{1}}\right\rangle}.

In order that the above inequality holds for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, we get
{\left\langle{h_{1}},{\mathbf{S}_{12}g_{2}}\right\rangle}=0
, which proves the claim.

From the claim above, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{S}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}).

Additionally, since
\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
and
\mathbf{S}_{21}\subset\mathbf{S}_{12}^{*}
, we have
\mathbf{S}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}=\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{S}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{*}]\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}.

However, this entails that the range of (LHS) in (
50
) belongs to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\perp}
, whereas the range of the (RHS) is included in
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
. Therefore, we conclude
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}=0
, which is a contradiction. This also incorporates that
\mathbf{S}_{21}=\mathbf{T}_{21}
, so
\mathbf{T}_{21}
is closable.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Constructing a concrete s.p.d. extension is a delicate issue. To illustrate the difficulty, recall the block components of the optimal pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}
from (
1
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{11}=\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2},\quad\mathbf{T}_{21}=(\mathbf{C}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}_{12})^{*}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}.

where
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
is the block s.p.d. operator in (
(

⇐

)
‣
6.2
),
A natural candidate for the s.p.d. extension is the operator
\mathbf{S}
formed by analogy with the finite-dimensional solution (
3.3
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{T}_{11}&\mathbf{T}_{21}^{*}\\
\mathbf{T}_{21}&(\mathbf{C}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}_{12})^{*}\mathbf{C}_{11}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{C}_{11}^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{C}_{12})\end{pmatrix}.

However, this formal construction is problematic – verifying whether this is even densely defined is intractable.

6.3
Kantorovich Geodesics

Given covariance operators
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, we may assume
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
without loss of generality due to
Corollary

6.6
. As in
Section

4.3
, we consider

\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}):=\argmin_{\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})}(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}),\quad t\in[0,1].

Obviously,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}=\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{1}=\mathbf{B}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators with
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
and
t\in[0,1]
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=\left\{\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{G}_{t}\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}:\mathbf{G}_{t}=(1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M},\,\mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B}),\ \mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}\right\}\neq\emptyset,

which does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.8
.

We first show that (RHS) is not an emptyset. Assume
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
from
Corollary

6.6
. Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
, where
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is an optimal pre-pushforward. Then, we get
\mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B})
and
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
. Note from
Lemmas

A.1
and
6.16
that
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\tr[\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}]=\tr[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{G})^{1/2}]=\tr[(\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

Once we establish the equivalence, it becomes clear that (RHS) does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
, as (LHS) does not depend on the choice of
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
:

(\supseteq)

For any
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
in (RHS), by direct calculation as in
Theorem

4.10
, we get
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=t\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. Then, invoking the proof of
Theorem

4.10
again, we get

\displaystyle(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})\geq t(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})=(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}),

for any
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, i.e.,
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
.

(\subseteq)

Given
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{B})
, and
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{C})
. Then,

\displaystyle(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}(1-t){|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{R}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+t{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}-\mathbf{R}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{R}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}-2\tr[\mathbf{R}^{*}((1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M})]+(1-t){|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+t{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}-{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+(1-t){|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}+t{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{M}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=t(1-t){|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{M}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}t(1-t)\tr[\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}-2(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}]=t(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}).

The equality holds in (c) if and only if
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
and the equality holds in (b) if and only if
\mathbf{R}=(1-t)\mathbf{G}+t\mathbf{M}
. In this case, the equality automatically holds in (a). Hence
\mathbf{C}
belongs to (RHS).

∎

Theorem 6.9

(Kantorovich Problem)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators with
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
. Then, the set of all operators
\mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B})
satisfying
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
[(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12}+\mathbf{N}_{12}]^{*}&\mathbf{M}_{22}\end{pmatrix}\ :\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array}\rightarrow\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{H}_{1}\\
\oplus\\
\mathcal{H}_{2}\end{array},

where:

•

\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
satisfies
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})
and
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
.

•

\mathbf{M}_{22}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
satisfies
\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}
.

A solution corresponds to a Monge map (i.e.,
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
for some optimal pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
) if and only if one chooses
\mathbf{M}_{22}=\mathbf{0}
(i.e.,
\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
).

Proof of
Theorem

6.9
.

Decompose
\mathbf{A}^{1/2}
and
\mathbf{M}
as:

\mathbf{A}^{1/2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}_{11}&\mathbf{M}_{12}\\
\mathbf{M}_{21}&\mathbf{M}_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

Repeating the proof of
Theorem

4.7
, we get
\mathbf{M}_{11}=\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}
and
\mathbf{M}_{12}=\mathbf{0}
, and it remains to solve:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{12},

(51)

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=\mathbf{B}_{22}.

(52)

As in the proof of
Theorem

6.5
, (
27
) always have a solution characterized by

\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{12})+\mathbf{N}_{12},\quad\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}((\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2})=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}).

The rest of the proof is then same to that of
Theorem

4.7
.
∎

Consequently,
Remark

4.2
holds if one replaces
(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{-1}
by
\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}
. In particular, for any
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
,
(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}
and
(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}
are fixed, and the off-diagonal blocks
(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}=(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}^{*}
only depends on the choice of
\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
, subject to the conditions in
Theorem

6.9
. To emphasize this dependence, we again write
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
. This leads to the counterpart of
Corollary

4.9
.

Corollary 6.10

(Uniqueness)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators. For any
t\in(0,1)
, the set of barycenters
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is a singleton if and only if the equivalent conditions in
Theorem

6.7
hold.
Furthermore, this unique barycenter is the McCann interpolant at time
t
.

Proof of
Corollary

6.10
.

Trivial from
Propositions

6.8
and
6.9
.
∎

Also, all the geodesics connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
can be constructed in a same fashion to
Theorem

4.10
:

Theorem 6.11

(Kantorovich Geodesics)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators with
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
.

1.

Any path
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H}),\,t\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
constructed by fixing a choice of
\mathbf{N}_{12}
in
Theorem

6.9
generates a constant-speed geodesic, that is,
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{s}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}},\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}})=(t-s)\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
for any
0\leq t\leq s\leq 1
.

2.

For any
t\in(0,1)
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, the
reverse
Schur complements vanish, i.e.,
\mathbf{A}/\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
.

3.

Conversely, any constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
is of the form
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
for some valid choice of
\mathbf{N}_{12}
in
Theorem

6.9
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.11
.

1.

Same to the proof of
Theorem

4.10
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{G}_{t}\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, where
\mathbf{G}_{t}=(1-t)\mathbf{A}^{1/2}+t\mathbf{M}
with
\mathbf{M}\in\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{B}),\ \mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
. To show
\mathbf{A}/\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*})=\{\mathbf{0}\},

due to
Lemma

2.4
. Let
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*})
. Then, there exists some
\mathbf{d}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{d}
and

\displaystyle 0=\mathbf{d}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{d}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{t}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1}\mathbf{d}=(1-t)\mathbf{d}^{*}[\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\mathbf{M}]\mathbf{d}+t\mathbf{c}^{*}\mathbf{c}\geq t\|\mathbf{c}\|^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{0}.

Consequently, we get
\mathbf{A}/\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
. Repeating this with
\mathbf{B}
, we get
\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}=\mathbf{0}
.

3.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be a constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
. Fix
t\in(0,1)
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+t\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})\geq\left(\frac{1}{1-t}+\frac{1}{t}\right)^{-1}(\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})+\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}))^{2}\geq t(1-t)\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}).

From the proof of
Proposition

6.8
, the equality holds if and only if
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. This shows that
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{t}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
for some
\mathbf{N}_{12}
given in
Theorem

6.9
. Moving forward, from part 2 and

Corollary

6.10
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}:[t,0]\to\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
(reversed time) is the unique constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
and
\mathbf{A}
. Similarly,
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}:[t,1]\to\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
is the unique constant-speed geodesic connecting
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}
and
\mathbf{B}
.

∎

Analogous to Villani’s quote
[
51
]

“a geodesic in the space of laws is the law of a geodesic,”
our findings for Gaussian measures can be summarized as:
“A geodesic in the space of Green operators is the Green operator of a geodesic”
. Finally, we establish an infinite-dimensional counterpart to
Theorem

4.8
. While we show that every McCann interpolant is an extreme point of the set of barycenters, the converse remains an open question, especially due to (Case 2) in the proof of
Theorem

4.8
.

Proposition 6.12

(Extreme Geodesics)
.

Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators with
\mathbf{A}\leadsto\mathbf{B}
. The set of barycenters
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, which is a closed convex set, has the following properties:

1.

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is a McCann interpolant at time
t
, then it is an extreme point of
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
.

2.

For any barycenter
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, we have
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{\mathbf{N}_{12}}/\mathbf{A}=t^{2}(\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12})
.

3.

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is a McCann interpolant at time
t
if and only if
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.12
.

1.

Repeating the proof of
Theorem

4.8
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:=\left\{\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}\mid\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}),\ \mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}\preceq\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}\right\},

is a closed convex set. Then, any
\mathbf{N}_{12}:\mathcal{H}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
with
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{N}_{12})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{B}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}),\ \mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}=\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}
is an extreme point of
\mathcal{A}
since the equality in (
29
) holds if and only if
\mathbf{P}_{0}=\mathbf{P}_{1}\in\mathcal{E}
.
Then, from
Remark

4.2
, it follows that
\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is the closed convex set. It also follows that
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{K}_{t}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is an extreme point if and only if
\mathbf{N}_{12}\in\mathcal{A}
is an extreme point, hence a McCann interpolant at time
t
is an extreme point due to
Theorem

6.9
.

2.

Recall
\mathbf{T}_{11}=\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{11}^{\dagger/2}:\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
from
Theorem

6.5
, which is an s.p.d. operator due to
Proposition

6.3
. Using the block decomposition as in the proof of
Theorem

6.9
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}=\mathbf{X}_{11}\mathbf{X}_{11}^{*},\quad(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{11}[t\mathbf{M}_{21}]^{*},\quad(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}=t^{2}\mathbf{B}_{22}=t^{2}[\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}],

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{11}:=[(1-t)\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}+t\mathbf{M}_{11}]=[(1-t)\mathbf{I}_{11}+t\mathbf{T}_{11}]\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}.

Note that
\mathbf{X}_{11}
is injective because
\mathbf{X}_{11}f=0
implies

\displaystyle 0={\left\langle{\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}f},{\mathbf{X}_{11}f}\right\rangle}\geq(1-t)\|\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}f\|^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{A}_{11}^{1/2}f=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad f=0.

To ease the notation, let us denote

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{11}:=[(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}+t\mathbf{M}_{11}]=[(1-t)\mathbf{G}_{11}+t(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*})^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{11}\mathbf{G}_{11})^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{11}^{-1}]\mathbf{G}_{11},

which is injective. Then, the
\mathbf{A}
-Schur complement of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}/\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{22}-\overline{(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{21}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{11}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t})_{12}}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{21}\overline{\left[\mathbf{I}_{11}-\mathbf{X}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{X}_{11}\mathbf{X}_{11}^{*})^{\dagger}\mathbf{X}_{11}\right]}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}

\displaystyle=t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{22}\mathbf{M}_{22}^{*}+t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{21}\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{X}_{11})}\mathbf{M}_{21}^{*}=t^{2}\mathbf{M}_{22}=t^{2}[\mathbf{B}/\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{N}_{12}^{*}\mathbf{N}_{12}].

3.

This is an immediate consequence of part 2 and
Theorem

6.9
.

∎

6.4
Convex Analysis Perspective

We revisit our results through the lens of convex analysis in
Kantorovich dual formulation. Let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
, and
\mathbf{G}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}),\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{B})
with
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}\succeq\mathbf{0}
. This entails that
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{G}
so that
{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}},{\mathbf{M}z}\right\rangle}
for any
z,\tilde{z}\in\mathcal{H}
. Also, the displacement convexity reads

\displaystyle(1-t){\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z},{\mathbf{M}z}\right\rangle}+t{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}}\right\rangle}-{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}((1-t)z+t\tilde{z})},{\mathbf{M}((1-t)z+t\tilde{z})}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=t(1-t){\left\langle{\mathbf{G}(z-\tilde{z})},{\mathbf{M}(z-\tilde{z})}\right\rangle}\geq 0.

(53)

Define a closed linear subspace in
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}
by
\mathcal{S}:=\overline{\{(\mathbf{G}z,\mathbf{M}z):z\in\mathcal{H}\}}^{\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}}
.
In other words,
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}
if and only if there exists a sequence
\{z_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
such that
\mathbf{G}z_{n}\to x
and
\mathbf{M}z_{n}\to y
. We remark that
\mathcal{S}
is a graph if and only if there exists a closable optimal pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}
such that
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}
, and in this case,
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{G}(\overline{\mathbf{T}})
. Moving forward, we consider the sections of
\mathcal{S}
, which are closed affine subspaces by the continuity of projection:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x):=\{y\in\mathcal{H}:(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}\},\quad\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{G}}(y):=\{x\in\mathcal{H}:(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}\}.

Lemma 6.13
.

For any
x\in\mathcal{H}
with
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\neq\emptyset
,
y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\mapsto{\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}/2\in\mathbb{R}

is a constant function.

Proof of
Lemma

6.13
.

Let
y,\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{S}(x)
. Then, there exist sequences
\{z_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
and
\{\tilde{z}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}z_{n},\mathbf{M}z_{n})\to(x,y),\quad(\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}_{n},\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n})\to(x,\tilde{y}).

Accordingly,

\displaystyle{\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}-{\left\langle{x},{\tilde{y}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}[{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{\mathbf{M}z_{n}}\right\rangle}-{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}_{n}},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n}}\right\rangle}]

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}[{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}(z_{n}-\tilde{z}_{n})},{\mathbf{M}z_{n}}\right\rangle}+{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}(z_{n}-\tilde{z}_{n})},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n}}\right\rangle}]={\left\langle{0},{y}\right\rangle}+{\left\langle{0},{\tilde{y}}\right\rangle}=0.

∎

From the lemma above, the following functions
\phi,\psi:\mathcal{H}\to[0,+\infty]
are well-defined:

\displaystyle\phi(x):=\begin{cases}{\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}/2&,\quad y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\neq\emptyset,\\
+\infty&,\quad\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)=\emptyset.\end{cases},\quad\psi(y):=\begin{cases}{\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}/2&,\quad x\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{G}}(y)\neq\emptyset,\\
+\infty&,\quad\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{G}}(y)=\emptyset.\end{cases}.

Proposition 6.14
.

The function
\phi:\mathcal{H}\to[0,+\infty]
(resp.
\psi:\mathcal{H}\to[0,+\infty]
) is convex. Their subdifferentials at
x\in\mathcal{H}
(resp.
y\in\mathcal{H}
) satisfies
\partial\phi(x)\supset\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)
(resp.
\partial\psi(y)\supset\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{G}}(y)
).

Proof of
Proposition

6.14
.

To show the convexity of
\phi
, we fix
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{H}
and
t\in(0,1)
, and demonstrate

\displaystyle(1-t)\phi(x)+t\phi(\tilde{x})\geq\phi((1-t)x+t\tilde{x}).

We may assume
y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\neq\emptyset
and
\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(\tilde{x})\neq\emptyset
, otherwise trivial. Then, there exist sequences
\{z_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
and
\{\tilde{z}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}z_{n},\mathbf{M}z_{n})\to(x,y),\quad(\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}_{n},\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n})\to(\tilde{x},\tilde{y}),

and thus
(1-t)y+t\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{S}((1-t)x+t\tilde{x})
. Consequently,
Lemma

6.13
and (
6.4
) lead to

\displaystyle\phi((1-t)x+t\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}((1-t)z_{n}+t\tilde{z}_{n})},{\mathbf{M}((1-t)z_{n}+t\tilde{z}_{n})}\right\rangle}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left[(1-t)\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{\mathbf{M}z_{n}}\right\rangle}}{2}+t\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}_{n}},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n}}\right\rangle}}{2}\right]=(1-t)\phi(x)+t\phi(\tilde{x}).

We now show that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\subset\partial\phi(x)
. It suffices to show that for any
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}
, it holds that
\phi(x+\delta_{x})\geq\phi(x)+{\left\langle{y},{\delta_{x}}\right\rangle}
for any
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{H}
. We may assume that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(\delta_{x})\neq\emptyset
, otherwise trivial. Choose
\delta_{y}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(\delta_{x})
. Then, there exist sequences
\{z_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
and
\{\tilde{z}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{G}z_{n},\mathbf{M}z_{n})\to(x,y),\quad(\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}_{n},\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n})\to(\delta_{x},\delta_{y}).

Consequently,
(x+\delta_{x},y+\delta_{y})\in\mathcal{S}
, thus
Lemma

6.13
and (
6.4
) lead to

\displaystyle\phi(x+\delta_{x})-\phi(x)-{\left\langle{y},{\delta_{x}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}(z_{n}+\tilde{z}_{n})},{\mathbf{M}(z_{n}+\tilde{z}_{n})}\right\rangle}}{2}-\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{\mathbf{M}z_{n}}\right\rangle}}{2}-{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}\tilde{z}_{n}},{\mathbf{M}\tilde{z}_{n}}\right\rangle}}{2}=\phi(\delta_{x})\geq 0.

∎

As a direct consequence, we get
\phi(x)+\psi(y)={\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}
whenever
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}
. If we consider the Gaussian coupling
\pi
of
\mu=N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{A})
and
\nu=N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{B})
with its covariance

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{G}\mathbf{M}^{*}\\
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{G}^{*}&\mathbf{B}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}\\
\mathbf{M}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{G}^{*}&\mathbf{M}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}),

then
\text{supp}(\pi)=\mathcal{S}
due to
Lemma

5.1
, and the classical results from Kantorovich dual formulation again confrims that
\pi
is indeed optimal
[
51
,
4
]
. However, the Fenchel’s strong duality
[
47
]
between
\phi
and
\psi
does not hold in general.

Theorem 6.15
.

The convex conjugate of the function
\phi:\mathcal{H}\to[0,+\infty]
is given by

\displaystyle\phi^{c}(y)=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{H}}[{\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}-\phi(x)]=\begin{cases}\|(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}^{*}y\|^{2}/2&,\quad\mathbf{G}^{*}y\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}],\\
+\infty&,\quad\mathbf{G}^{*}y\notin\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}].\end{cases}

Proof of
Theorem

6.15
.

We first show that
\phi^{c}(y)<+\infty
only if
\mathbf{G}^{*}y\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]
by contradiction.

(Step 1)

We claim
\mathbf{G}^{*}y\in\overline{\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]}
. If not, let
z\neq 0
be the projection of
\mathbf{G}^{*}y
onto
\overline{\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]}
. Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
t(\mathbf{G}z,\mathbf{M}z)\in\mathcal{S}
, thus
Lemma

6.13
yields

\displaystyle\phi^{c}(y)\geq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}[t{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z},{y}\right\rangle}-\phi(\mathbf{G}z)]=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}[t{\left\langle{z},{\mathbf{G}^{*}y}\right\rangle}-\|(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z\|^{2}/2]=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}t\|z\|^{2}=+\infty,

which is a contradiction.

(Step 2)

From (Step 1), the following linear functional is well-defined:

\displaystyle l_{y}:\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]\to\mathbb{R},(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z\mapsto{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z},{y}\right\rangle}

By our assumption, we also have for any
0\neq\omega\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]
that

\displaystyle+\infty>\phi^{c}(y)=\sup_{z\in\mathcal{H}}[{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z},{y}\right\rangle}-\phi(\mathbf{G}z)]\geq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left[l_{y}(t\omega)-\frac{\|t\omega\|^{2}}{2}\right]=\frac{l_{y}(\omega)^{2}}{2\|\omega\|^{2}},

hence
|l_{y}(\omega)|\leq\sqrt{2\phi^{c}(y)}\|\omega\|
, i.e.
l_{y}
is bounded. Due to the Riesz representation theorem, there exists a unique
z_{y}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]}
such that
l_{y}(\omega)={\left\langle{z_{y}},{\omega}\right\rangle}
for any
\omega\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]
. Consequently, for any
z\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle{\left\langle{z},{\mathbf{G}^{*}y}\right\rangle}=l_{y}((\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z)={\left\langle{z_{y}},{(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{z},{(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{y}}\right\rangle},

i.e.,
\mathbf{G}^{*}y=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{y}\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]
.

Conversely, assume
\mathbf{G}^{*}y\in\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]
, and define
z_{y}:=(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{\dagger/2}\mathbf{G}^{*}y\in\overline{\mathcal{R}[(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}]}
. We claim that
\phi^{c}(y)=\|z_{y}\|^{2}/2
.

(
\leq
)

Fix any
x\in\mathcal{H}
that has a non-trivial effective domain, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)\neq\emptyset
. Then, there exist
y_{x}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)
and a sequence
\{z_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
such that
(\mathbf{G}z_{n},\mathbf{M}z_{n})\to(x,y_{x})
. Hence,

\displaystyle{\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}-\phi(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{y}\right\rangle}-\frac{{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{\mathbf{M}z_{n}}\right\rangle}}{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\left\langle{(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{n}},{z_{y}}\right\rangle}-\frac{\|(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{n}\|^{2}}{2}\leq\frac{\|z_{y}\|^{2}}{2}.

(
\geq
)

Choose a sequence
\{z_{n}\in\mathcal{H}\}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{n}=z_{y}
. Since
(\mathbf{G}z_{n},\mathbf{M}z_{n})\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\phi^{c}(y)

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}[{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}z_{n}},{y}\right\rangle}-\phi(\mathbf{G}z_{n})]=\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\left\langle{(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{n}},{z_{y}}\right\rangle}-\frac{\|(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{n}\|^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|z_{y}\|^{2}}{2}-\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|(\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M})^{1/2}z_{n}-z_{y}\|^{2}}{2}=\frac{\|z_{y}\|^{2}}{2}.

∎

Example 6.1

(Particle Movement)
.

We consider
\mathbf{A}=\diag(1,0)
and
\mathbf{B}=\diag(0,1)
from
Example

3.2
. From
Examples

4.2
and
4.10
, any geodesics connecting
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
can be parametrized by
s\in[-1,+1]
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}^{s}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2},\,t\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{t}^{s}=\begin{pmatrix}(1-t)^{2}&t(1-t)s\\
t(1-t)s&t^{2}\end{pmatrix},

and the extremals
s=\pm 1
correspond to Monge geodesics with the OT matrices
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{\pm}
(or
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{A}}^{\pm}
) in
Example

3.2
. We remark that when
|s|<1
, i.e., Kantorovich geodesics, there are infinite choices of corresponding optimal couplings: for instance, when
s=0
,

•

Independent Coupling:
\pi=\mu\otimes\nu
with

\displaystyle\text{supp}(\pi)=\mathcal{S}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}:x_{2}=0\right\}\times\left\{\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}:y_{1}=0\right\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

•

Gaussian Mixture:
\pi=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{+})\sharp\mu+\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}}^{-})\sharp\mu

•

Convex combinations of above couplings.

However, regardless of the choice of
s
, the corresponding Green matrices always yield
\mathbf{G}^{*}\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{0}
due to
Corollary

4.5
, hence they all have the same corresponding convex functions:

\displaystyle\phi\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{cases}0&,\quad x_{2}=0,\\
+\infty&,\quad x_{2}\neq 0.\end{cases},\quad\psi\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{cases}0&,\quad y_{1}=0,\\
+\infty&,\quad y_{1}\neq 0.\end{cases}.

In this case, they are convex conjugate to one another:
\phi(x)+\psi(y)={\left\langle{x},{y}\right\rangle}
if and only if
(x,y)\in\mathcal{S}
.

6.5
Comments on Barycenters

The nullity of an operator is not necessarily same to that of the adjoint when
\dim\mathcal{H}=\infty
. Consequently, unlike in the finite-dimensional case, the set of all Green’s operators
\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A})
cannot be generated by applying unitary transforms to a single Green’s operator, while it can be by partially isometric transform (
Lemma

2.2
). This fact invalidates
Lemma

4.1
, a cornerstone of the barycenter theory developed in
Sections

4.1
and
4.2
. Its infinite-dimensional counterpart, presented below, highlights the crucial differences:

Theorem 6.16
.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
be covariance operators. For any
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{1})
and
\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\tr[(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}]=\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]=\sup\{\tr(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}):\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{I}\}.

The supremum is achieved as a maximum if and only if
\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2})=\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{2}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1})
.

Proof of
Theorem

6.16
.

(Step 1)

We show that
\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]
is independent of the choice of
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{1})
. Let
\mathbf{G}_{1}=\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
be the polar decomposition, where
\mathbf{U}
is a unique partial isometry with initial space
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{1})^{\perp}
and final space
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{\perp}
. Then,
\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}
, hence

\displaystyle[\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})\mathbf{U}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1},

hence
(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}=\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}\mathbf{U}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]=\tr[\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}]=\tr[\mathbf{\Pi}_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}]=\tr[(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})^{1/2}].

(Step 2)

We claim that
\sup\{\tr(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}):\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{I}\}\geq\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]
(the other direction is trivial from
Lemma

A.1
), using finite rank approximation. Note that
\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
since
\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H})
. Consider its SVD decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k}e_{k}\otimes f_{k},

where
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\dots>0
are positively-valued non-increasing sequence of singular values,
\{f_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an ONB of
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2})}
, and
\{e_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an ONB of
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{G}_{2}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1})}
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider a unitary operator
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathbf{U}_{n}e_{k}=f_{k}
for
k=1,2,\cdots,n
. Then,
\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathcal{H})
so

\displaystyle\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}|{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{n}e_{k}},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}|\leq\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k+1}|{\left\langle{\mathbf{U}_{n}e_{k}},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}|\leq\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k+1}\to 0,\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty,

which results in

\displaystyle\tr(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{n}e_{k}},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{k}+\sum_{k=n+1}^{\infty}{\left\langle{\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{n}e_{k}},{e_{k}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\to\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k}={|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{1}=\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}],\quad\text{ as }n\to\infty.

(Step 3)

Given a unitary operator
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, we have
\tr(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U})=\tr[(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}]
if and only if
\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\mathbf{U}=(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{G}_{1})^{1/2}
, which is equivalent to
\mathbf{U}e_{k}=f_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. This happens if and only if
\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2})=\dim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{G}_{2}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{1})

[
10
]
.

∎

This result has direct implications for the Procrustes formulation of the Wasserstein distance. As correctly characterized in
[
43
,
35
]
, the Procrustes distance must be defined via an infimum:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2})=\inf\{{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}-\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}:\mathbf{U}^{*}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{I}\},

(54)

However, contrary to a popular misconception, this infimum is not always achieved as a minimum when
\dim\mathcal{H}=\infty
. This is not a rare pathology; the following proposition demonstrates that for any injective covariance operator, one can always construct a second operator for which the minimum is not attained, although the reachability is satisfied.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
\dim\mathcal{H}=\infty
. For any injective covariance operator
\mathbf{A}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
, there exists another covariance operator
\mathbf{A}_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
such that
\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}
is injective while
\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}
is not. In this case, the infimum in (
54
) cannot be attained by any unitary operator
\mathbf{U}
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.17
.

Since
\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
is a compact operator, it cannot be surjective due to the open mapping theorem
[
10
]
. Choose
v\notin\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})
with
\|v\|=1
, and define

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{2}:=\mathbf{\Pi}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{H}),\quad\mathbf{\Pi}=\mathbf{I}-v\otimes v.

Note that
\mathbf{\Pi}
is a projection with
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{\Pi})=\spann\{v\}
and
\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}=\mathbf{\Pi}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{\Pi}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
. It is straightforward that
v\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2})
, hence
\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}
is not injective.

We claim that
\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}
is injective. Let
f\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})
. Then,

\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}f\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})\cap\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2})=\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2})\cap\spann\{v\}=\{0\}.

This shows that
\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}f=0
, thus
f=0
, i.e.,
\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}
is injective. Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(\mathbf{A}_{1},\mathbf{A}_{2})<{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{A}_{1}^{1/2}-\mathbf{A}_{2}^{1/2}\mathbf{U}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{2}^{2}

for any unitary operator
\mathbf{U}
due to
part 1 in
Theorem

6.16
.
∎

If
\mathbf{A}_{1}\leadsto\mathbf{A}_{2}
with an optimal pre-pushforward
\mathbf{T}
from
\mathbf{A}_{1}
to
\mathbf{A}_{2}
, then for any
\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{1})
,
\mathbf{G}_{2}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathbf{A}_{2})
is a Green’s operator satisfying
\mathbf{G}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{G}_{2}\succeq\mathbf{0}
.

7
Gaussian Processes

This section demonstrates how to find optimal pre-pushforwards between Gaussian processes using the theory of integral operators and their kernels.
Let
(E,\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a measure space and
\mathcal{H}:=L_{2}(E,\mu)
, where
E
is a compact set and
\mu
is a finite measure. For a bivariate function
G\in L_{2}(E\times E,\mu\times\mu)
, we define the corresponding integral operator by

\mathscr{K}_{G}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\quad\mathscr{K}_{G}f(s):=\int_{E}G(s,t)f(t)\mathrm{d}\mu(t),\quad f\in\mathcal{H}:=L_{2}(E,\mu).

The bivariate function
G^{*}(s,t)=G(t,s)
is called the
adjoint kernel
, as
(\mathscr{K}_{G})^{*}=\mathscr{K}_{G^{*}}
. The composition of two integral operators corresponds to the box product of their kernels, defined as

G_{1}\square G_{2}:E\times E\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad(G_{1}\square G_{2})(s,t):=\int_{E}G_{1}(s,u)G_{2}(u,t)\mathrm{d}\mu(u),

which satisfies
\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}\square G_{2}}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}
. In particular,
K=G\square G^{*}
is an s.p.d. kernel. Conversely, Mercer’s theorem
[
37
]
states that for any such continuous kernel, there exists a bivariate function
G
, called the
Green’s function
such that
K=G\square G^{*}
, which allows us to define the RKHS
[
42
]
as

\mathbb{H}(K):=\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K}_{G}),\quad\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}:=\|\mathscr{K}_{G}^{\dagger}f\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

When
G
is also chosen to be a continuous s.p.d. kernel, it is denoted by
G=K^{1/2}
as
\mathscr{K}_{G}=(\mathscr{K}_{K})^{1/2}
. Unless
\mathbb{H}(K)
is finite-dimensional, it is
not
a closed subspace in
\mathcal{H}
with respect to the
L_{2}
-norm.

To treat a Gaussian process
X
on
E
as a random element in
\mathcal{H}
, we assume it is jointly measurable. For Gaussian processes, this property can be easily verified from the regularity of its covariance kernel
K
via Kolmogorov’s continuity theorem
[
13
,
26
,
21
]
. For such a process, the integral operator
\mathscr{K}_{K}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
serves as its covariance
operator

[
24
]
.

Numerous covariance kernels of interest are generated by a linear differential operator
\mathscr{L}
with boundary conditions. The Green’s function
G
for such an operator is defined as the distributional solution to
\mathscr{L}G=\delta
in the weak sense, where
\delta
is the Dirac delta function
[
22
,
10
]
. This relationship implies that
\mathscr{L}
acts as the pseudoinverse of the integral operator
\mathscr{K}_{G}
:

\mathscr{L}\mathscr{K}_{G}f(s)=\mathscr{L}\int_{E}G(s,t)f(t)\mathrm{d}\mu(t)=\int_{E}\delta(s,t)f(t)\mathrm{d}\mu(t)=f(s).

Thus, we get
\mathscr{L}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
where
K=G\square G^{*}
, and can be identified with
\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{K}_{G}^{\dagger}
. This perspective suggests that Green’s functions, which are often non-symmetric, are more fundamental objects than the covariance kernels they generate.

Example 7.1

(Brownian Motion and Bridge)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=L_{2}[0,1]
and consider the differential operators

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{1}f=f^{\prime},\,f(0)=0,\qquad\mathscr{L}_{2}f=f^{\prime},\,f(0)=f(1)=0.

The corresponding Green’s functions are the non-symmetric:

G_{1}(s,t)=I_{[0,s]}(t),\quad G_{2}(s,t)=I_{[0,s]}(t)-s,\quad 0\leq s,t\leq 1,

These generate the well-known covariance kernels for Brownian motion (BM) and a Brownian bridge (BB), respectively:
K_{1}(s,t)=\min(s,t)
and
K_{2}(s,t)=\min(s,t)-st
for
0\leq s,t\leq 1
.
Note that they are jointly measurable Gaussian, and their associated integral operators are given by

(\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}f)(s)=\int_{0}^{s}f(t)\mathrm{d}s,\quad(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}f)(s)=\int_{0}^{s}f(t)\mathrm{d}t-s\int_{0}^{1}f(t)\mathrm{d}t,\quad 0\leq s\leq 1,

which results in

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}})=\mathbb{H}(K_{1})=\left\{f:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\left.\right|f\text{ is absolutely continuous},\ f(0)=0,\,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}})=\mathbb{H}(K_{2})=\left\{f:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\left.\right|f\text{ is absolutely continuous},\ f(0)=f(1)=0,\,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\right\},

which are dense due to the Stone-Weierstrass theorem.
It is straightforward that
\mathscr{L}_{1}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}^{\dagger}
and
\mathscr{L}_{1}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}^{\dagger}
are just usual differentiations, hence

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{2}=\mathscr{L}_{1}\circ\imath_{2\to 1}:\mathbb{H}(K_{2})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\quad\imath_{2\to 1}:\mathbb{H}(K_{2})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}(K_{1}),f\mapsto f

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{1}=\mathscr{L}_{2}\circ\imath_{1\leadsto 2}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(K_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\quad\imath_{1\leadsto 2}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(K_{2}),f\mapsto f(\cdot)-\cdot f(1),

(55)

to match the boundary conditions.
Importantly,
\imath_{1\leadsto 2}
is not bounded.
Using integration by parts,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{L}_{1}^{*})=\mathbb{H}(K_{1}),\quad\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{L}_{2}^{*})=\left\{f:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\left.\right|f\text{ is absolutely continuous},\,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\right\}.

In particular,
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{L}_{2}^{*})
has no boundary condition as we have put too many boundary conditions on
\mathscr{L}_{2}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be two jointly measurable Gaussian processes with covariance kernels
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
and corresponding Green’s functions
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
. Assume that
\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{K_{1}})=\{0\}
. Then the unique optimal pre-pushforward from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}
is the s.p.d. operator

\mathbf{S}_{X_{1}\leadsto X_{2}}:=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}^{*}}^{\dagger}\mathscr{K}_{(G_{1}^{*}\square K_{2}\square G_{1})^{1/2}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}^{\dagger}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}.

Proof of
Proposition

7.1
.

Since
(\mathscr{K}_{G})^{*}=\mathscr{K}_{G^{*}}
and
\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}\square G_{2}}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}
, the result follows from
Proposition

6.3
.
∎

Applying this proposition directly can be cumbersome, as shown by the following corollary.

Corollary 7.2
.

For a BM (
X_{1}
) and a BB (
X_{2}
), the optimal pre-pushforwards to one another are

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{X_{1}\leadsto X_{2}}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\ f\mapsto\left(\mathscr{K}_{K_{121}^{1/2}}f^{\prime}\right)^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{X_{2}\leadsto X_{1}}:\mathbb{H}(K_{2})\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\ f\mapsto\left(\mathscr{K}_{K_{212}^{1/2}}f^{\prime}\right)^{\prime},

where the s.p.d. kernels
K_{121},K_{212}\succeq 0
are complicated integral kernels

\displaystyle K_{121}:[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad K_{121}(s,t)=\int_{s}^{1}\int_{t}^{1}(\min(u,v)-uv)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v,

\displaystyle K_{212}:[0,1]\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad K_{212}(s,t)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}(I_{[s,1]}(u)-u)\min(u,v)(I_{[t,1]}(v)-v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

Proof of
Corollary

7.2
.

Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be their associated Green’s functions, respectively, as in
Example

7.1
. Note that the differntial operators
\mathscr{L}_{i}^{*}=-\mathscr{L}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, so it suffices to show that
K_{121}=G_{1}^{*}\square K_{2}\square G_{1}
and
K_{212}=G_{2}^{*}\square K_{1}\square G_{2}
:

\displaystyle(G_{1}^{*}\square K_{2}\square G_{1})(s,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}G_{1}(u,s)K_{2}(u,v)G_{1}(v,t)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}I_{[s,1]}(u)K_{2}(u,v)G_{1}I_{[t,1]}(v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v=\int_{s}^{1}\int_{t}^{1}(\min(u,v)-uv)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v,

and similarly,

\displaystyle(G_{2}^{*}\square K_{1}\square G_{2})(s,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}(I_{[s,1]}(u)-u)\min(u,v)(I_{[t,1]}(v)-v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

∎

The square roots of the kernels
K_{121}
and
K_{212}
are difficult to compute analytically. Fortunately, a simpler method often exists.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
be two jointly measurable Gaussian processes with the Green’s functions
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.
Assume that
G_{1}^{*}\square G_{2}\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}})\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}})
. Then,
X_{1}\leadsto X_{2}
, and an optimal pre-pushforward is given by:

\mathbf{T}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(K_{2}),\,\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}f\mapsto\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}f,\quad f\in\mathcal{H}.

Proof of
Theorem

7.3
.

Denote by
\mathbf{A}=\mathscr{K}_{K_{1}}
,
\mathbf{G}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}
, and
\mathbf{B}=\mathscr{K}_{K_{2}}
.
Then
\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}
satisfies (
32
) and (
33
):

\displaystyle(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})^{*}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}^{*}=\mathscr{K}_{K_{2}}=\mathbf{B},\quad\mathbf{G}^{*}(\mathbf{T}\mathbf{G})=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}^{*}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}^{*}\square G_{2}}\succeq\mathbf{0},

thus
\mathbf{T}
is an optimal pre-pushforward.
∎

Provided that
\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}})=\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}})
, this procedure automatically generates an optimal pre-pushforward in the other direction, as
G_{1}^{*}\square G_{2}\succeq 0
implies
G_{2}^{*}\square G_{1}\succeq 0
:

Example 7.2

(Integrated Brownian Motion)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=L_{2}[0,1]
and consider the differential operator
\mathscr{L}_{2}f=f^{\prime\prime}
with boundary conditions
f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)=0
. The corresponding Gaussian process is called the Integrated Brownian Motion (IBM), denoted
X_{2}
, which is jointly measurable. Its Green’s function
G_{2}

generates the RKHS:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}})=\mathbb{H}(K_{2})=\left\{f:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\left.\right|f\text{ is absolutely continuous},\ f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)=0,\,f^{\prime},f^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}\right\},

which are dense.

Let
X_{1}
be a BB and
X_{2}
be an IBM. The optimal pre-pushforward from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}
and vice versa are unique since
\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}})=\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}})=\{0\}
due to
Theorem

6.4
. Also,
Theorem

6.4
shows that
X_{1}\to X_{2}
as
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}})\subset\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}})
, while
X_{2}\leadsto X_{1}
and
X_{2}\nrightarrow X_{1}
. In this case, denoting its definite integral by
F(t)=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\mathrm{d}s
, the condition
G_{1}^{*}\square G_{2}\succeq 0
can be verified easily:

\displaystyle{\left\langle{h},{\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}^{*}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}h}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle={\left\langle{\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}h},{\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}h}\right\rangle}={\left\langle{F},{\int_{0}^{\cdot}F(s)\mathrm{d}s}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}F(t)\left(\int_{0}^{t}F(s)\mathrm{d}s\right)\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}F(s)\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}\geq 0,\quad f\in\mathcal{H}.

Therefore, the unique optimal pre-pushforwards are given by
Theorem

7.3
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{X_{1}\leadsto X_{2}}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\,\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}f\mapsto\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}f,\quad f\in\mathcal{H},

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{X_{2}\leadsto X_{1}}:\mathbb{H}(K_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\,\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}f\mapsto\mathscr{K}_{G_{2}}f,\quad f\in\mathcal{H},

which are are simply integration and differentiation upon extension:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{X_{1}\to X_{2}}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{1}}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\,f\mapsto\int_{0}^{\cdot}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\tilde{\mathbf{T}}_{X_{2}\leadsto X_{1}}=\mathscr{L}_{1}:\mathbb{H}(K_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\,f\mapsto f^{\prime}.

This reasoning extends naturally to higher-order Integrated Brownian Motions (IBMs). We identify that optimal pre-pushforwards between IBMs correspond to multiple integration and differentiation operators. To the best of our knowledge, this constitutes the first result providing an explicit value for the 2-Wasserstein distance between infinite-dimensional Gaussian measures.

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=L_{2}[0,1]
, and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider a differential opertaor
\mathscr{L}_{n}f=f^{(n)}
with boundary conditions
f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)=\cdots=f^{(n-1)}(0)=0
, and
X_{n}
be the associated Gaussian process, called the
n
-th IBM, which is jointly measurable.
Then for any
n\leq m
, the optimal pre-pushforwards between
n
-th and
m
-th IBMs are given by
(m-n)
-fold integration and differentiation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}_{X_{n}\to X_{m}}=\mathscr{K}_{G_{m-n}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}),\quad\mathbf{T}_{X_{m}\leadsto X_{n}}=\mathscr{L}_{m-n}:\mathbb{H}(K_{m-n})\subset\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},

Hence,
X_{n}\to X_{m}
,
X_{m}\leadsto X_{n}
, and
X_{m}\nrightarrow X_{n}
. Moreover, the squared 2-Wasserstein distance between them are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}^{2}(X_{n},X_{m})=\begin{cases}C_{n}+C_{m}-2C_{(n+m)/2}&,\quad n+m\text{ is even}\\
C_{n}+C_{m}-C_{(n+m+1)/2}&,\quad n+m\text{ is odd}\end{cases},\quad C_{n}=\frac{1}{2n(2n-1)[(n-1)!]^{2}}.

Proof of
Corollary

7.4
.

By the Stone-Weierstrasse theorem,
\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K}_{G_{n}})=\{0\}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, it suffices to show that
\mathscr{K}_{G_{n}^{*}}\mathscr{K}_{G_{m}}\succeq 0
. For any
h\in\mathcal{H}
, if
n+m
